<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


We resolve the regular-point bi-Lipschitz conjecture in the noncollapsed setting. For every integer <i>n</i> ≥ 2 and every real <i>K</i>, every regular point of a noncollapsed $`\mathrm{RCD}(K,n)`$ space has an open neighborhood bi-Lipschitz homeomorphic to an open subset of ℝ<sup><i>n</i></sup>. The bi-Lipschitz constant depends only on <i>n</i>, and the neighborhood uses the restricted ambient distance.

| [stmt-re-field-estimates](#stmt-re-field-estimates) | lemma | `re:field-estimates` | 26 | At $P=P_x$, $x\in B_1(p)$, write $r=r_i$, $H=H_i(x)$, $Q=Q_i(x)$, $Z=Z_i(x)$, $S=S_i(x)$, and $E=E_i(x)$. |
| [stmt-re-inverse-majorant](#stmt-re-inverse-majorant) | lemma | `re:inverse-majorant` | 27 | Fix $\sigma\ge1$ and put $L_0=1$ and $$L_m=(m+1)((m+1)!)^\sigma\qquad(m\ge1).$$ Then |
| [stmt-re-jet-step](#stmt-re-jet-step) | lemma | `re:jet-step` | 28 | Choose $B\ge C_v$ sufficiently large and then $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, depending on $A,\sigma,B$ and the fixed data. |

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

Euclidean tangent spaces describe the geometry of a metric space at a point, but a coordinate chart must compare all pairs of points in a neighborhood. Under a lower Ricci curvature bound these are distinct regularity questions.

Throughout, noncollapsed means that the reference measure is exactly $\mathcal H_d^n$. We use the full $\mathrm{CD}(K,n)$ condition together with quadratic Cheeger energy. Spaces are complete, separable, geodesic, and have full-support reference measure finite on bounded sets. A point is *$n$-regular* if every pointed Gromov–Hausdorff tangent there is pointed-isometric to $(\mathbb R^n,|\cdot|,0)$. Here a tangent is a pointed limit of rescalings by inverse radii tending to infinity. We prove the following open-neighborhood result.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every integer $n\ge2$ there is a constant $L_n\ge1$ with the following property. Let $K\in\mathbb R$, and let $(X,d,\mathcal H_d^n)$ be a noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}(K,n)$ space. Let $p\in X$. If every pointed Gromov–Hausdorff tangent of $X$ at $p$ is pointed-isometric to $(\mathbb R^n,|\cdot|,0)$, then there exist an open neighborhood $U$ of $p$, an open set $V\subset\mathbb R^n$, and a homeomorphism $F:U\to V$ such that $$L_n^{-1}d(x,z)\le |F(x)-F(z)|\le L_nd(x,z)
 \qquad (x,z\in U).$$ The distance on the left and right is the original distance $d$ restricted to $U$.*

</div>

The neighborhood may depend on $X$ and $p$; the distortion constant depends only on the dimension. The theorem concerns every prescribed regular point, not only almost every point of the reference measure. It also yields the following manifold region.

<a id="stmt-cor-full-measure-atlas"></a>
<div id="cor:full-measure-atlas" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *Under the hypotheses of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>, there is an open set $\mathcal U\subset X$ containing every $n$-regular point, with $\mathcal H_d^n(X\setminus\mathcal U)=0$, and a countable atlas of $L_n$-bi-Lipschitz charts from ambient-open subsets of $\mathcal U$ onto open subsets of $\mathbb R^n$. Each chart uses the restricted ambient distance, and its transition maps are $L_n^2$-bi-Lipschitz.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-full-measure-atlas"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-full-measure-atlas</summary>

*Proof.* The $n$-regular set has full $\mathcal H_d^n$ measure by (De Philippis and Gigli 2018, Theorem 1.12(ii),(vi)) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18)], applied with reference measure $\mathcal H_d^n$. Take $\mathcal U$ to be the union of the chart domains in Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. As a separable metric space, $\mathcal U$ is second countable, so these domains admit a countable subcover. On an overlap, composing one chart with the inverse of the other gives the stated transition bound. ◻

</details>

The open region $\mathcal U$ may contain points outside the exact regular locus; the corollary does not assert that this locus is open.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### History and significance

The structure theory of noncollapsed limits of Riemannian manifolds with lower Ricci bounds provides an early form of this question. Cheeger and Colding obtained manifold neighborhoods with bi-Hölder parametrizations and asked about a bi-Lipschitz upgrade (Cheeger and Colding 1997, Theorem 5.14 and Remark 5.15) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[CC97](#ref-CC97)]. Their question concerns quantitatively regular Ricci-limit neighborhoods and is a historical antecedent of the synthetic regular-point problem considered here.

Synthetic lower Ricci bounds were developed through optimal transport by Lott and Villani and by Sturm (Lott and Villani 2009; Sturm 2006) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[LV09](#ref-LV09), [Sturm06II](#ref-Sturm06II)]. The Riemannian refinement of Ambrosio, Gigli and Savaré distinguishes quadratic Cheeger energy and linear heat flow (Ambrosio, Gigli, and Savaré 2014) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[AGS14](#ref-AGS14)]; the finite-dimensional connections with Bochner inequalities were developed by Erbar, Kuwada and Sturm (Erbar, Kuwada, and Sturm 2015) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[EKS15](#ref-EKS15)]. In the noncollapsed setting, De Philippis and Gigli established volume convergence, volume rigidity, and quantitative Euclidean approximation (De Philippis and Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18)].

Mondino and Naber proved countable bi-Lipschitz rectifiability of RCD spaces: measurable pieces cover almost every point and map to measurable Euclidean subsets (Mondino and Naber 2019, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[MN19](#ref-MN19)]. This does not give an ambient-open neighborhood of each prescribed regular point. Kapovitch and Mondino proved that the entire regular set is contained in an open manifold region with bi-Hölder charts (Kapovitch and Mondino 2021, Theorem 3.1 and Corollary 3.2) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[KM21](#ref-KM21)]. This statement does not require the exact regular locus itself to be open. An open bi-Lipschitz chart requires stronger control across infinitely many scales.

The conjecture that every regular point in the noncollapsed setting admits a bi-Lipschitz chart is explicitly recalled in the recent work of Honda and Zhang (Honda and Zhang 2026, sec. 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[HZ26](#ref-HZ26)]. Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> answers this question under the precise noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}(K,n)$ hypotheses stated above. Honda and Zhang’s geometric regularity theorems assume a positive lower bound for injectivity radius on neighborhoods (Honda and Zhang 2026, Theorems 1.1 and 3.35) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[HZ26](#ref-HZ26)]. Their analytic criterion also permits Morrey $L^2$ control of the Laplacians, together with a neighborhood-uniform positive Gram determinant (Honda and Zhang 2026, Theorem 3.9 and Proposition 3.10) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HZ26](#ref-HZ26)]. Neither additional hypothesis is assumed here.

Several nearby results clarify the strength of the conclusion. Colding and Naber proved that bounded subsets of quantitative Reifenberg loci in noncollapsed Ricci limits, with sufficiently small flatness and scale parameters, admit bi-Lipschitz embeddings into a Euclidean space of possibly higher dimension (Colding and Naber 2013, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[CN13](#ref-CN13)]. They also constructed examples with Euclidean tangents that admit no coordinates inducing a Hölder continuous metric tensor (Colding and Naber 2013, Theorem 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[CN13](#ref-CN13)]. The conclusion here is an open chart into $\mathbb R^n$; it does not assert Hölder continuity of the metric tensor in that chart. Likewise, the restrictions on bi-Lipschitz regular maps in (Honda and Peng 2023, Proposition 5.1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[HP23](#ref-HP23)] concern maps whose pullbacks of smooth target test functions have locally bounded Laplacians (Honda and Peng 2023, Definition 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[HP23](#ref-HP23)]. No such analytic regularity is asserted for the limiting coordinates constructed below; in particular, their components are not asserted to have bounded Laplacian.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The two main estimates

After rescaling at $p$, volume comparison makes all sufficiently small balls at nearby centers quantitatively Euclidean. Harmonic functions approximating Euclidean coordinates then give an open coordinate map $u=(u_1,\ldots,u_n)^T$. The scale-dependent mean Gram matrices of $u$ are invertible, but they need not be uniformly nondegenerate as the scale tends to zero. We compensate for this degeneration in the *space of coordinate values*.

Two kinds of control make this possible. Accumulated Bochner mass records how much the coordinates must be stretched to compensate for their degeneration; this accumulation may be unbounded. A separate estimate evaluates the same mass forms on actual coordinate differences $u(x)-u(z)$ and controls them by defects whose sum is small. The construction uses the cumulative mass to prescribe the stretching and the directional estimate to keep its metric error summable.

Write $r_i=2^{-i}$, and let $G=(\langle\nabla u_j,\nabla u_k\rangle)_{j,k=1}^n$ be the Gram matrix field. Its mean on $B_{r_i}(y)$ is denoted by $R_i(y)$. Let $\mathfrak m$ denote Hausdorff measure in the current length units. If the rescaled curvature lower bound is $-\kappa$, the matrix-valued Bochner measure is $M=\tfrac12\Delta G+\kappa G\mathfrak m$, with the Laplacian understood entrywise. For a fixed enlargement $L>1$, define the positive semidefinite matrix $$D_{i,y}=R_i(y)^{-1}
       \bigl[r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Lr_i}(y))\bigr]R_i(y)^{-1},
 \qquad D[\xi]=\xi^TD\xi.$$ Corrected heat averages of $G$ yield positive definite forms $H_i(x)$, increasing with $i$, on the space of coordinate values. Lemma <a href="#co:cumulative-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:cumulative-comparison">14</a> compares these forms with accumulated bounded-overlap sums of the local matrices $D_{i,y}$, including a reverse comparison for suitable weighted sums. This is the cumulative input to the construction.

The first main estimate gives directional control of the same local matrices. For each fixed $L,C_{\mathrm{sep}}>1$, there are nonnegative defect functions $E_i$ on the working region such that, whenever $x,z,y\in B_4(p)$ and $d(x,y),d(z,y)\le C_{\mathrm{sep}}r_i$, $$D_{i,y}[u(x)-u(z)]
 \le C r_i^2\bigl(E_i(x)+E_i(z)\bigr),
 \qquad \sup_{x\in B_4(p)}\sum_{i\ge0}E_i(x)\longrightarrow0$$ as the initial rescaling becomes flat (Proposition <a href="#pi:directional-pinching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:directional-pinching">24</a>). The proof uses Poisson replacements of squared distance, affine approximation of radial derivatives, and a sharp projection estimate for the difference of two replacements. A radial transport argument preserves the *squared* defect in this last estimate. Poisson replacement, harmonic transformation, heat Gram normalization, and sharp squared-Hessian pinching have substantive antecedents in the work of Cheeger and Colding and of Cheeger, Jiang and Naber (Cheeger and Colding 1996; Cheeger, Jiang, and Naber 2021) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[CC96](#ref-CC96), [CJN21](#ref-CJN21)]. The two-center directional bound displayed above is proved here in the form needed for the value-space construction.

Scale-dependent transformations are also central in (Cheeger and Naber 2015, Theorem 1.32) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[CN15](#ref-CN15)] and (Huang and Huang 2024, Theorems 1.7 and 4.1) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[HH24](#ref-HH24)]. Potentially unbounded, noncommuting normalization matrices already occur in these methods and in (Honda and Zhang 2026, Theorem 3.8) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[HZ26](#ref-HZ26)]; those freedoms alone do not remove the Hölder loss. The issue here is how to use the directional estimate to control the cumulative metric error without assuming summability of its square root.

The second main ingredient is the analytic construction in Proposition <a href="#re:metric-iteration" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:metric-iteration">29</a>. On the common open value domain $\Omega=u(B_1(p))$, the construction produces smooth maps $h_i:\Omega\to\mathbb R^n$ whose ordinary Jacobians $Dh_i$ remain invertible. From the forms $D_{i,y}$ and smooth cutoffs we build covector fields $v_i$ and, for $P\in\Omega$, iterate $$h_0(P)=P,\qquad
 h_{i+1}(P)=h_i(P)+Dh_i(P)^{-T}v_i(P).$$ The leading change in the pullback metric is a positive sum of the forms $D_{i,y}$. When the inverse transpose is differentiated, its linear contribution to a squared length contains $D^2h_i(\xi,\xi)$. A recurrence for these diagonal derivatives allows the errors to be summed using $\sum_i E_i$, even when $\sum_i\sqrt{E_i}$ diverges. A separate estimate for all derivatives, with Gevrey growth in their order, closes the iteration despite the loss of one derivative in each update.

This part of the argument uses only positive matrix comparisons, directional smallness, and smooth cutoffs; it does not differentiate the scale metrics $H_i(x)$ or assume they remain bounded. It isolates an analytic way to turn summable directional information into coordinates, separate from the geometric origin of that information. The limit $F=\lim_i h_i\circ u$ has the asserted metric bounds. The openness of its image follows from the open coordinates $u$ and invariance of domain.

Summability also governs bi-Lipschitz Reifenberg parametrizations. For extrinsic sets, David and Toro control coherent changes of approximating planes by square-summable errors (David and Toro 2012, Proposition 8.34) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[DT12](#ref-DT12)]. For metric spaces, Gigli and Violo obtain bi-Lipschitz parametrizations under summability of ordinary dyadic Gromov–Hausdorff errors (Gigli and Violo 2025, Theorems 2.2 and 2.7) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[GV25](#ref-GV25)]. Regularity at a point does not itself supply that summability. Here the summable quantity is instead the directional Bochner defect, and its conversion into metric control is proved by the explicit iteration above.

Section <a href="#co:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:section">2</a> constructs the open harmonic domain and its scale normalizations. Section <a href="#co:heat-section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:heat-section">3</a> proves the cumulative matrix comparison, and Section <a href="#pi:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:section">4</a> proves the summable directional estimate. Section <a href="#re:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:section">5</a> uses these two inputs to construct the renormalization and prove the all-pairs metric bounds and openness of the limiting chart.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Conventions

All balls use the ambient metric. Every enlargement appearing in a local estimate is fixed before a sufficiently late rescaling is chosen. We write $C$ for constants depending on the dimension and on specified fixed enlargement factors. Uniform quantities tending to zero along the rescalings will be denoted by $o(1)$; this notation includes uniformity over the centers and all dyadic scales under consideration. A subscript, as in $o_A(1)$, permits dependence on constants fixed earlier.

For a positive definite matrix $A$, set $$A[\xi]=\xi^T A\xi,\qquad |\xi|_A=A[\xi]^{1/2}.$$ Matrix inequalities are inequalities of quadratic forms. Vectors and covectors are represented using the fixed Euclidean pairing, and their length norms are specified explicitly. The norm $\|T\|_{H\to Q}$ of a multilinear map uses $|\cdot|_H$ on each argument and $|\cdot|_Q$ on its value. Derivatives of maps on coordinate value space are ordinary Euclidean derivatives. We use $\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_B$ for the integral divided by the measure of $B$.

<a id="co:section"></a>
### Uniform harmonic coordinates

We first obtain an open coordinate domain. The estimates in this section allow the derivatives of these coordinates to degenerate as the scale decreases. Their degeneration will be measured by positive matrices in the next section.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Local calculus and rescaling

For locally Sobolev functions write $\Gamma(f,g)=\langle\nabla f,\nabla g\rangle$ and $\Gamma(f)=\Gamma(f,f)$. Laplacians of gradient squares will be understood as measures. Products with such measures always use the continuous representative of a Lipschitz function, or the capacity representative of a Sobolev function when that is the appropriate meaning of the pairing.

We write $g$ for the canonical metric tensor.

<a id="stmt-co-local-calculus"></a>
<div id="co:local-calculus" class="lemma">

<a id="co:M-definition"></a>
<a id="co:shifted-bochner"></a>
**Lemma 3** (Local analytic facts). *Let $(Y,d,\mathfrak m)$ be a noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}(-\kappa,n)$ space and let $\Omega\subset Y$ be open. A locally bounded solution of $\Delta w=a$ in $\Omega$, with $a$ constant, is locally Lipschitz and is a local test function. In particular it has a local $L^2$ Hessian, $\Gamma(w_1,w_2)\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}(\Omega)$ for two such functions, and the Hessian product rule holds locally. The measure $$\label{co:M-definition}
 M(w)=\frac12\Delta\Gamma(w)+\kappa\Gamma(w)\mathfrak m$$ is locally finite and nonnegative. For every constant $b$, $$\label{co:shifted-bochner}
 M(w)-(2b\Delta w-nb^2)\mathfrak m
 \ge |\operatorname{Hess}w-bg|^2\mathfrak m.$$ On the real vector space of local constant-Laplacian functions, the left side is a nonnegative quadratic form in the pair $(w,b)$, with both entries scaled together. All these statements hold on interior balls for weak solutions with locally bounded $L^2$ norm, after using the interior estimates to obtain local boundedness.*

<a id="co:dirichlet-sobolev"></a>
*On fixed interior scales, bounded Lipschitz cutoffs with bounded Laplacian are available. Under uniform local volume comparison and a uniform rescaled curvature bound, their bounds, local Poincare and Sobolev constants, and interior harmonic and Poisson estimates are uniform. In particular, on a fixed-radius ball in the rescaled spaces below there is a number $d_*>1$ and a constant $C$ such that $$\label{co:dirichlet-sobolev}
 \|h\|_{L^2}+\|h\|_{L^{2d_*}}
 \le C\|\nabla h\|_{L^2},\qquad h\in W^{1,2}_0(B),$$ whenever the ball and a fixed larger ball are contained in the working region.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-local-calculus"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-local-calculus</summary>

*Proof.* The full curvature-dimension condition implies the reduced condition used in some of the references, by (Bacher and Sturm 2010, Proposition 2.5(i)) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[BS10](#ref-BS10)]. The interior Poisson regularity and gradient estimates apply to a constant right hand side; see (Kell 2013; Jiang 2014) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Kell13](#ref-Kell13), [Jiang14](#ref-Jiang14)]. More precisely, if $8B_R(z)$ is compactly contained in the domain, the quantitative estimate in (Jiang 2014, Theorem 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Jiang14](#ref-Jiang14)] has the form $$\|\nabla w\|_{L^\infty(B_R(z))}
 \le C\bigl(R^{-1}+\sqrt{c_\kappa(R^2)}\bigr)
       \bigl(\|w\|_{L^\infty(B_{8R}(z))}
                  +R^2\|\Delta w\|_{L^\infty(B_{8R}(z))}\bigr).$$ Here $c_\kappa$ is the heat-semigroup variance constant in that estimate; the RCD curvature bound controls it, and the doubling and Poincare constants in $C$ are uniform on the rescaled balls in use. With bounded rescaled curvature this gives the usual $R^{-1}\|w\|_\infty+R|a|$ bound. The interior local-boundedness estimate first supplies the $L^\infty$ norm from an $L^2$ bound. Our later uses always leave the required eightfold interior room. The local test class has the characterization $$\operatorname{Test}_{\rm loc}(Y)
 =\{f\in D_{\rm loc}(\Delta)\cap L^\infty_{\rm loc}:
       |\nabla f|\in L^\infty_{\rm loc},\ 
       \Delta f\in W^{1,2}_{\rm loc}\},$$ with the analogous definition on $\Omega$; see (Connell et al. 2021, Lemmas 2.6–2.7) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[CDNZPSW](#ref-CDNZPSW)]. Thus constant-Laplacian solutions satisfy the stated local test condition. Equivalently, one may first multiply by a cutoff to obtain Laplacian-domain and Hessian regularity, and then use a test cutoff on a smaller ball to obtain the local product calculus. The relevant Hessian and product rules are those of (Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Gigli18](#ref-Gigli18)].

The noncollapsed hypothesis implies that the tangent module has dimension $n$ almost everywhere, and that $\operatorname{tr}\operatorname{Hess}w=\Delta w$ there. The dimensional Bochner inequality therefore implies $M(w)\ge|\operatorname{Hess}w|^2\mathfrak m$; see (De Philippis and Gigli 2018; Han 2018) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18), [Han18](#ref-Han18)]. Expanding the square $$|\operatorname{Hess}w-bg|^2=|\operatorname{Hess}w|^2-2b\Delta w+nb^2$$ proves <a href="#co:shifted-bochner" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:shifted-bochner">[co:shifted-bochner]</a>. Local finiteness and the measure formulation follow from the same local test calculus. Polarization of a nonnegative quadratic form gives the last assertion about $(w,b)$.

The cutoff, Poincare, and Sobolev statements are local RCD estimates. For the heat-regularized cutoff construction, see (Mondino and Naber 2019, Lemma 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-28"></a>[[MN19](#ref-MN19)] and (Connell et al. 2021, Lemma 2.6) <a id="pm-cite-29"></a>[[CDNZPSW](#ref-CDNZPSW)]. To obtain the zero-boundary coercivity in <a href="#co:dirichlet-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:dirichlet-sobolev">[co:dirichlet-sobolev]</a>, extend $h$ by zero to a fixed larger ball. Poincare on that ball and the positive lower bound for the measure of its annulus give the $L^2$ estimate. The local Sobolev inequality followed by this estimate gives the other term. One may use an effective doubling dimension larger than both $n$ and $2$, so that $d_*>1$ also when $n=2$; see (Hajłasz and Koskela 2000, Theorem 5.1 and the following Remark 3) <a id="pm-cite-30"></a>[[HK00](#ref-HK00)]. The annulus lower bound and all constants are uniform for the almost Euclidean balls used below. ◻

</details>

<a id="co:matrix-measure-cs"></a>
For a column $u=(u_1,\ldots,u_n)^T$ of harmonic functions, define <a id="co:G-M-matrix"></a>$$\label{co:G-M-matrix}
 G=(\Gamma(u_j,u_k))_{j,k=1}^n,
 \qquad M=(M(u_j,u_k))_{j,k=1}^n,$$ where $M(\cdot,\cdot)$ denotes polarization of <a href="#co:M-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:M-definition">[co:M-definition]</a>. Thus $M[b]=M(b\cdot u)$ is a positive measure for every $b\in\mathbb R^n$. If $E$ is Borel, positivity implies $$\label{co:matrix-measure-cs}
 |M(v,w)|(E)\le \sqrt{M[v](E)M[w](E)}.$$ Here $|M(v,w)|$ is total variation. To see this, take the trace measure as a dominating measure and apply matrix Cauchy–Schwarz to the positive semidefinite matrix density. The same argument applies to integrals with a nonnegative weight.

We record precisely the rescaling regime used throughout the proof. $\omega_n$ denotes the measure of the unit ball in $\mathbb R^n$.

<a id="stmt-co-flat-setup"></a>
<div id="co:flat-setup" class="lemma">

<a id="co:uniform-flatness"></a>
<a id="co:original-gradient-bound"></a>
**Lemma 4** (An expanding almost Euclidean regime). *Fix an $n$-regular point $p$ in the space of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. There are rescalings $$(X_\nu,d_\nu,\mathfrak m_\nu,p_\nu)
 =(X,s_\nu^{-1}d,s_\nu^{-n}\mathcal H_d^n,p),
 \qquad s_\nu\downarrow0,$$ numbers $\kappa_\nu\downarrow0$, and radii $T_\nu\uparrow\infty$ with $\kappa_\nu T_\nu^2\to0$, having the following properties. The space $X_\nu$ is $\mathrm{RCD}(-\kappa_\nu,n)$. For a sequence $\delta_\nu\downarrow0$, all balls with center in $B_{64T_\nu}(p_\nu)$ and radius at most $64T_\nu$ satisfy $$\label{co:uniform-flatness}
 \left|\frac{\mathfrak m_\nu(B_r(y))}{\omega_n r^n}-1\right|
 \le\delta_\nu,
 \qquad
 d_{GH}\bigl(\overline B_r(y),\overline B_r(0^n)\bigr)
 \le\delta_\nu r.$$ There are harmonic columns $u_\nu$ on $B_{32T_\nu}(p_\nu)$, converging on every fixed ball locally uniformly and strongly in $W^{1,2}$ to Euclidean coordinates, and $$\label{co:original-gradient-bound}
 |\nabla u_\nu|\le C_n
 \quad\text{on }B_{16T_\nu}(p_\nu).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-flat-setup"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-flat-setup</summary>

*Proof.* Take rescalings tending to zero. Compactness and the regular-point hypothesis imply pointed convergence to $\mathbb R^n$, after passage to a subsequence, and noncollapsed volume convergence identifies the limit measure with $\mathcal H^n$; see (De Philippis and Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-31"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18)]. The rescaled curvature lower bound can be taken with $\kappa_\nu=\max\{-K,0\}s_\nu^2$, enlarged by an arbitrarily small positive number if necessary.

Fix for the moment a large finite radius. At every bounded center, volumes of balls at a fixed positive radius converge to their Euclidean values. This convergence is uniform in the center: otherwise a subsequence of centers converges in the Euclidean limit and contradicts volume convergence at that limit center. For a noncollapsed RCD space the volume density is at most one at every point, not merely almost everywhere. Bishop–Gromov therefore traps every smaller model-normalized volume ratio between its almost maximal value at the fixed radius and one. The model-volume correction tends uniformly to zero because the rescaled curvature does. This proves the first assertion in <a href="#co:uniform-flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:uniform-flatness">[co:uniform-flatness]</a>, uniformly over all smaller radii. The almost-volume-rigidity theorem in (De Philippis and Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-32"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18)], applied on a fixed enlargement of the ball, proves the second assertion. Its interior-radius conclusion is why an enlargement is taken first. No openness of the set of exactly regular points is used.

Interior harmonic approximation to Euclidean coordinate functions is available by (Ambrosio and Honda 2018, Corollary 4.12) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[AH18](#ref-AH18)]. For each fixed large radius construct the harmonic functions on a strictly larger ball. Interior energy convergence, local boundedness, and the interior gradient estimate give convergence to coordinates and a dimensional gradient bound on the smaller ball. The latter bound is independent of the fixed large radius: the coordinate functions have size $O(r)$ on a ball of radius $r$, and the interior gradient estimate includes the factor $r^{-1}$.

Finally use a diagonal subsequence, allowing the finite radii just chosen to increase sufficiently slowly. It can be chosen so that all the estimates hold up to the multiples of $T_\nu$ displayed above, with $\delta_\nu\to0$ and $\kappa_\nu T_\nu^2\to0$. This gives <a href="#co:original-gradient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:original-gradient-bound">[co:original-gradient-bound]</a> and the expanding domains of harmonicity simultaneously. ◻

</details>

From now on the sequence index is suppressed. Every assertion on a fixed ball, or with finitely many fixed enlargement factors, is understood to hold for all sufficiently large $\nu$. A quantity denoted by $o(1)$ is uniform in the centers in that ball and in all the smaller scales. We set <a id="co:dyadic-R"></a>$$\label{co:dyadic-R}
 r_i=2^{-i},\qquad
 R_i(y)=\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_{r_i}(y)}G\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m,
 \qquad i=0,1,\ldots.$$

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Transformation at every center and scale

Harmonic normalization across scales is a central tool in (Cheeger and Naber 2015, Theorem 1.32) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[CN15](#ref-CN15)] <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[CJN21](#ref-CJN21)], (Cheeger, Jiang, and Naber 2021, sec. 7), and (Huang and Huang 2024, Theorem 4.1). The noncollapsed RCD transformation framework of Bruè–Naber–Semola (Bruè, Naber, and Semola 2020, sec. 3.3, Proposition 3.13 and Corollary 3.16) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[BNS20](#ref-BNS20)] is a further methodological antecedent. We give the all-center statement and its proof needed on the expanding domains used here.

<a id="stmt-co-harmonic-normalization"></a>
<div id="co:harmonic-normalization" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Uniform harmonic normalization). *For every pair of fixed constants $A,C\ge1$, the matrices $R_i(y)$ are positive definite for $y\in B_A(p)$ and $i\ge0$. In the rescaled ball $B_{Cr_i}(y)$, the map <a id="co:normalized-column"></a>$$\label{co:normalized-column}
 U_{i,y}=\frac{R_i(y)^{-1/2}(u-u(y))}{r_i}$$ is, after an orthogonal change of target coordinates, an $o(1)$ approximation to Euclidean coordinates, uniformly and strongly in interior $W^{1,2}$. Its gradients are uniformly bounded on each fixed interior enlargement. Its Gram matrix converges strongly in every finite $L^p$ on such enlargements to $\mathrm{Id}$.*

<a id="co:nearby-R"></a>
<a id="co:slow-R"></a>
<a id="co:R-lower"></a>
*For every fixed $C\ge1$, if $d(y,z)\le Cr_i$ and $C^{-1}r_i\le r_j\le Cr_i$, then $$\label{co:nearby-R}
 (1-o(1))R_i(y)\le R_j(z)\le(1+o(1))R_i(y).$$ For every $a>0$, after choosing a sufficiently late rescaling, $$\label{co:slow-R}
 C_a^{-1}2^{-2a|i-j|}R_j(y)
 \le R_i(y)\le C_a2^{2a|i-j|}R_j(y).$$ The constants are uniform in the center and the two indices. In particular, since $R_0(y)=\mathrm{Id}+o(1)$ on fixed balls, $$\label{co:R-lower}
 c_a r_i^{2a}\mathrm{Id}\le R_i(y)\le C\mathrm{Id}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-harmonic-normalization"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-harmonic-normalization</summary>

*Proof.* We give the compactness induction, including the order of the smallness choices. Say that a harmonic column is $\tau$-normalizable at $(y,r)$ if there is an invertible matrix $T$ for which $T(u-u(y))/r$, on $B_{2r}(y)$ in units $r$, differs by at most $\tau$ from a Euclidean coordinate map under a Gromov–Hausdorff approximation. Replacing the uniform error in this definition by an interior normalized $L^2$ error gives an equivalent induction, after changing its tolerance by a fixed factor; we use harmonic interior estimates on a slightly larger ball whenever needed. Rotations of the Euclidean coordinates are allowed.

Choose a small fixed tolerance $\tau$. In an exactly Euclidean ball, a harmonic column $V$ that is $\tau$-close to the coordinate functions has $$\|DV(0)-\mathrm{Id}\|\le C_n\tau,
 \qquad
 \sup_{B_{2b}}|DV(0)^{-1}(V-V(0))-x|
 \le C_n\tau b^2.$$ These follow by applying interior derivative estimates to $V-x$. Choose a dyadic number $b=2^{-m}$ so small that $2C_n b<1/4$, and then choose $\tau$ small enough that the inverse derivative is bounded. Thus affine renormalization improves the error in units $b$ by a strict factor.

The same propagation holds on all sufficiently flat balls in our sequence. Indeed, if it failed, rescale a failing ball to unit size and apply its assumed normalization. The spaces converge to a Euclidean ball by <a href="#co:uniform-flatness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:uniform-flatness">[co:uniform-flatness]</a>. The normalized harmonic columns have uniform local $L^2$, energy, and gradient bounds and therefore converge locally uniformly and strongly in interior $W^{1,2}$ to a Euclidean harmonic column. These are interior convergence statements from (Ambrosio and Honda 2018, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[AH18](#ref-AH18)]; no convergence of a full boundary-value problem is required. The limit has the $\tau$-normalizability property. Its inverse derivative supplies a fixed matrix on the sequence which gives error strictly less than $\tau$ at radius $b$, a contradiction. This additional correction to the already normalized target, and its inverse, have norm at most $1+C_n\tau$, after allowing the orthogonal target changes in the definition.

At each bounded center the initial normalizability is supplied by Lemma <a href="#co:flat-setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:flat-setup">4</a>, uniformly in that center. Run the preceding step down each of the finitely many dyadic residue classes modulo $m$. This proves normalizability at every dyadic scale. The mean Gram matrix of a normalized column on its unit ball is $\mathrm{Id}+O(\tau)$, uniformly once the space is sufficiently flat: a contrary sequence again converges strongly in energy to a Euclidean harmonic column, whose derivative is $O(\tau)$-close to $\mathrm{Id}$ on that interior ball. Consequently these Gram matrices are nonsingular and replacing an inductively chosen $T$ by the canonical normalization $R_i(y)^{-1/2}$ changes the normalized map only by an orthogonal map and an $O(\tau)$ error.

To see explicitly why fixed enlargements are permitted, fix their largest factor first. Start the preceding induction on a larger fixed radius, allowing finitely many negative dyadic indices. For any scale $r_i$, an ancestor radius contains the desired enlargement, and at most a fixed number of the contraction steps separate that ancestor from $r_i$. Their normalizing matrices and inverse matrices are uniformly comparable. Apply the interior estimates on the ancestor ball and identify the mean Gram normalization on the descendant ball by strong energy convergence. All factors and the number of steps are fixed before $\tau$ and the geometric flatness are chosen. This proves normalizability and bounded normalized gradients on every fixed enlargement. As $\tau$ is arbitrary, the errors tend uniformly to zero along the original sequence, including when the chosen center and scale depend on the sequence index.

The same argument on an enlargement containing both balls in <a href="#co:nearby-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:nearby-R">[co:nearby-R]</a> proves that their Gram averages in the first normalization both tend to $\mathrm{Id}$. This gives <a href="#co:nearby-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:nearby-R">[co:nearby-R]</a>. Iterating its same-center adjacent-scale case, with error chosen so that each factor is at most $2^{2a}$, gives <a href="#co:slow-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-R">[co:slow-R]</a>. The upper bound in <a href="#co:R-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:R-lower">[co:R-lower]</a> also follows directly from <a href="#co:original-gradient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:original-gradient-bound">[co:original-gradient-bound]</a>.

For completeness, the strong convergence of the Gram functions can be seen without assuming convergence of pointwise gradient representatives. A Bochner cutoff estimate first bounds the local normalized Hessian energy. The Hessian product rule and bounded normalized gradients then bound the Gram functions in local $W^{1,2}$. Sobolev compactness and the convergence of integrals of gradient scalar products identify their limit with $\mathrm{Id}$; see (Ambrosio and Honda 2018, Corollary 4.3 and Theorem 4.6) <a id="pm-cite-38"></a>[[AH18](#ref-AH18)]. Their uniform boundedness upgrades the resulting strong $L^2$ convergence to every finite $L^p$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-bochner-small"></a>
<div id="co:bochner-small" class="lemma">

<a id="co:small-mass"></a>
**Lemma 6** (Small normalized Bochner mass). *For each pair of fixed constants $A,C\ge1$, uniformly for $y\in B_A(p)$ and $i\ge0$, $$\label{co:small-mass}
 r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Cr_i}(y))\le o(1)R_i(y).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-bochner-small"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-bochner-small</summary>

*Proof.* In units $r_i$, apply Lemma <a href="#co:local-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:local-calculus">3</a> to a scalar combination of $U=U_{i,y}$. The rescaled Bochner matrix is <a id="co:M-scaling"></a>$$\label{co:M-scaling}
 M_{r_i}(U)=r_i^{2-n}R_i(y)^{-1/2}M(u)R_i(y)^{-1/2},$$ where the curvature term in its definition is $\kappa r_i^2$. Choose a nonnegative cutoff equal to one on $B_C$ and supported on a larger fixed ball. Its gradient and Laplacian are uniformly bounded. For a fixed vector $b$, $$\int\phi\,\mathrm dM_{r_i}(b\cdot U)
 =\frac12\int\bigl(\Gamma(b\cdot U)-|b|^2\bigr)
                  \Delta\phi\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m_{r_i}
  +\kappa r_i^2\int\phi\Gamma(b\cdot U)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m_{r_i}.$$ Here $\int\Delta\phi\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m_{r_i}=0$. Both terms tend uniformly to zero by Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a>. Positivity of the Bochner measure bounds its mass on $B_C$. Polarization in the fixed finite dimension, or the same argument for unit vectors, gives the matrix bound. Conjugating back proves <a href="#co:small-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:small-mass">[co:small-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Open coordinate images

<a id="stmt-co-open-coordinates"></a>
<div id="co:open-coordinates" class="lemma">

<a id="co:ellipsoid-containment"></a>
<a id="co:separation-scale"></a>
**Lemma 7** (Open harmonic coordinates). *For every fixed $A\ge1$, the map $u$ is a homeomorphism from $B_A(p)$ onto an open subset of $\mathbb R^n$, once the rescaling is sufficiently late. There is a fixed $c>0$ such that, whenever the balls involved stay in a fixed interior working region, $$\label{co:ellipsoid-containment}
 \{P:|P-u(x)|_{R_i(x)^{-1}}<c r_i\}
 \subset u(B_{r_i/2}(x)).$$ Moreover, for every fixed $D\ge1$, if $D^{-1}r_i\le d(x,z)\le Dr_i$, then $$\label{co:separation-scale}
 \bigl||u(z)-u(x)|_{R_i(x)^{-1}}-d(x,z)\bigr|
 \le o_D(1)r_i.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-open-coordinates"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-open-coordinates</summary>

*Proof.* The approximation in Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a> gives <a href="#co:separation-scale" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:separation-scale">[co:separation-scale]</a>. Test a pair of distinct points at a dyadic scale comparable with its distance. Once the error is less than one half of that distance, the coordinate values are distinct. Distances larger than the initial unit scale in a fixed ball are separated directly by the initial uniform Euclidean approximation. Thus $u$ is injective on $B_A(p)$.

We prove <a href="#co:ellipsoid-containment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:ellipsoid-containment">[co:ellipsoid-containment]</a> with its interior margin. Fix a target $P$ in the indicated ellipsoid and put $x_0=x$, $\rho_0=r_i$. Write $R(\rho,y)$ for the Gram average at a dyadic radius. Suppose inductively that $$|P-u(x_j)|_{R(\rho_j,x_j)^{-1}}<c\rho_j,
 \qquad \rho_j=4^{-j}\rho_0.$$ Approximate surjectivity of the normalized Euclidean coordinate map provides $x_{j+1}$ with $$d(x_j,x_{j+1})\le(c+\eta)\rho_j,
 \qquad
 |P-u(x_{j+1})|_{R(\rho_j,x_j)^{-1}}\le\eta\rho_j.$$ Here $\eta\to0$ is uniform in the scale. For example, choose a point under the Gromov–Hausdorff correspondence whose Euclidean coordinate is the normalized target. Its distance from the center and the error in its coordinate value have the displayed bounds. By <a href="#co:nearby-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:nearby-R">[co:nearby-R]</a>, the second bound is at most $2\eta\rho_j$ when measured in $R(\rho_j/4,x_{j+1})^{-1}$. Choose $c<1/8$, and then choose $\eta<c/8$. It is at most $c\rho_{j+1}$, so the induction continues.

The total displacement is at most $(c+\eta)\sum_j\rho_j<\rho_0/2$. All centers therefore stay in the region where the estimates are valid. Completeness gives a limit $x_\infty\in B_{\rho_0/2}(x)$, with a strict margin because of the preceding choices. The upper bound $R(\rho,y)
\le C\mathrm{Id}$ in the original coordinate units shows that the Euclidean residual $|P-u(x_j)|$ tends to zero. Continuity of $u$ now gives $u(x_\infty)=P$.

Apply the ellipsoid statement at each point with a sufficiently small radius contained in $B_A(p)$. Its image is open. Finally, a continuous injection is a homeomorphism locally on each compact interior ball, since these spaces are proper. Alternatively the same separation-scale estimate gives continuity of the inverse. This proves the assertion on the whole open domain. ◻

</details>

<a id="co:heat-section"></a>
### Heat averages and cumulative matrix control

We next replace the ball Gram averages by matrices that are monotone as the spatial scale decreases. The local mass forms use inverse ball averages at nearby centers. Relative comparability of these averages with the heat averages does not by itself compare the twice-conjugated mass forms. We will bound the accumulated cost of changing the normalization by a small multiple of the final inverse heat matrix. Heat Gram normalization and quadratic telescoping are used in (Cheeger, Jiang, and Naber 2021, secs. 8.1–8.2) <a id="pm-cite-39"></a>[[CJN21](#ref-CJN21)]. The estimates below give the cumulative comparison for the inverse matrices without a commutativity assumption.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Continuous kernels and compactly supported sources

<a id="co:kernel-bounds"></a>
<a id="co:kernel-lower"></a>
Let $p_t(x,z)$ be the common continuous heat kernel and $P_t$ the heat semigroup. We use the Gaussian and first spatial gradient estimates of (Jiang, Li, and Zhang 2016, Theorem 1.2 and Corollary 1.2) <a id="pm-cite-40"></a>[[JLZ16](#ref-JLZ16)]. On the fixed testing regions, for $0<t\le1$, volume comparison allows their form $$\label{co:kernel-bounds}
 p_t(x,z)\le C t^{-n/2}e^{-d(x,z)^2/(Ct)},
 \qquad
 |\nabla_xp_t(x,z)|\le C t^{-(n+1)/2}e^{-d(x,z)^2/(Ct)}.$$ The gradient statement initially holds almost everywhere. The lower bound, used when $d(x,z)\le A\sqrt t$, is $$\label{co:kernel-lower}
 p_t(x,z)\ge c_A t^{-n/2}.$$ We may use one fixed weaker negative curvature bound in these estimates. The constants are then uniform in the sequence.

The following interpretation will be useful because $M$ can have a singular part.

<a id="stmt-co-kernel-measures"></a>
<div id="co:kernel-measures" class="lemma">

<a id="co:short-kernel"></a>
<a id="co:long-kernel"></a>
<a id="co:semigroup-measure"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Kernel differences and measure sources). *Fix $C_0\ge1$, $x,y$ in a fixed testing region, and $d(x,y)\le C_0r$, where $0<r\le1$. Put $$K_t(z)=\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_r(y)}p_t(a,z)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m(a),
 \qquad D=d(x,z).$$ After enlarging fixed constants, the following bounds hold at every source pole $z$: $$\begin{aligned}
 K_t(z)&\le C r^{-n}e^{-cD^2/r^2},&&0<t\le r^2,
       \label{co:short-kernel}\\
 |K_t(z)-p_t(x,z)|&\le C r t^{-(n+1)/2}e^{-cD^2/t},
       &&r^2\le t\le1.\label{co:long-kernel}
\end{aligned}$$ If $f\in W^{1,2}\cap L^\infty$ has compact support and its Laplacian is a finite signed measure $\mu$ of compact support, then $$\label{co:semigroup-measure}
 P_t f-P_s f=\int_s^t P_\tau\mu\,\mathrm d\tau,
 \qquad 0<s<t,$$ using the continuous representatives. In the application $f=\chi\Gamma(u_j,u_k)$, this identity can also be averaged over a ball and passed to $s\downarrow0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-kernel-measures"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-kernel-measures</summary>

*Proof.* When $D\le 2(C_0+2)r$, symmetry and total heat mass bound the integral of $p_t(a,z)$ over the ball by one. Dividing by its volume gives $Cr^{-n}$, which is the first bound with adjusted constants. If $D>2(C_0+2)r$, then $d(a,z)\ge D/2$ for all $a\in B_r(y)$. The Gaussian upper bound gives $Ct^{-n/2}e^{-cD^2/t}$. For $t\le r^2$ and $D/r$ bounded below by this fixed constant, its small-time power is absorbed in the exponential, giving <a href="#co:short-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:short-kernel">[co:short-kernel]</a>.

For the second bound, $d(a,x)\le(C_0+1)r$. Integrate the first spatial gradient estimate along a minimizing curve from $x$ to $a$; that curve stays in a fixed testing enlargement. The inequality $d(b,z)^2\ge D^2/2-d(b,x)^2$, with $t\ge r^2$, absorbs the variation of the Gaussian along the curve. This gives <a href="#co:long-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:long-kernel">[co:long-kernel]</a>, initially for almost every fixed source pole $z$. For each such pole, local Sobolev-to-Lipschitz gives the difference inequality for the continuous representative in the first variable. By symmetry, continuity of the common heat kernel, and full support, a sequence of these full-measure poles converges to any prescribed $z$. Passing to the limit, with relaxed Gaussian constants if necessary, proves the bounds at every pole. No gradient representative has been paired with a singular measure.

For <a href="#co:semigroup-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:semigroup-measure">[co:semigroup-measure]</a>, first test the distributional Laplacian against positive-time heat regularizations of compactly supported test functions. Symmetry and integration by parts give the asserted identity in distributions. Equivalently, differentiate $P_\tau f$ for positive $\tau$, pair the Laplacian with the continuous Sobolev function $p_\tau(x,\cdot)$, and integrate. A cutoff equal to one on the compact supports justifies this pairing; the kernel belongs locally to $W^{1,2}$ at positive time. The measure and its Sobolev-dual realization agree on this continuous representative. The kernel bounds give continuity of both sides in $x$, so the identity holds pointwise.

In our application the short-time averaged integral of the total variation of the source is finite: <a href="#co:short-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:short-kernel">[co:short-kernel]</a>, integrated up to $r^2$, bounds it by a finite Gaussian-weighted multiple of $r^{2-n}|\mu|$. Also $P_s f\to f$ in $L^1$ on the averaging ball. Thus the averaged identity passes to $s=0$ by absolute convergence. These statements also follow by first using positive lower time endpoints throughout and then applying the bounds just proved. ◻

</details>

<a id="co:cutoff-source"></a>
Choose a nonnegative cutoff $\chi$ equal to one on $B_T(p)$, supported in $B_{2T}(p)$, with $$|\nabla\chi|\le C/T,\qquad |\Delta\chi|\le C/T^2,$$ where $T=T_\nu$ comes from Lemma <a href="#co:flat-setup" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:flat-setup">4</a>. Extend $\chi G$ by zero outside its support. Its distributional Laplacian is the compactly supported matrix measure $$\label{co:cutoff-source}
 \Delta(\chi G)=2\chi M-2\kappa\chi G\mathfrak m+E_{\rm cut}\mathfrak m,
 \qquad E_{\rm cut}=(\Delta\chi)G+2\Gamma(\chi,G).$$ The last expression is a matrix of $L^1$ functions supported in the cutoff annulus.

<a id="stmt-co-heat-matrices"></a>
<div id="co:heat-matrices" class="lemma">

<a id="co:heat-derivative"></a>
<a id="co:QR-comparison"></a>
<a id="co:H-monotone"></a>
<a id="co:slow-QH"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Monotone heat Gram matrices). *There is $\lambda=\lambda_\nu>0$, with $\lambda_\nu\to0$, such that, at all fixed testing centers and $0<t\le1$, $$\label{co:heat-derivative}
 P_t(\chi M)
 \le\partial_t\bigl(P_t(\chi G)+\lambda t\mathrm{Id}\bigr)
 \le 3P_t(\chi M)+C\mathrm{Id}.$$ Define <a id="co:QH-definition"></a>$$\label{co:QH-definition}
 Q_i(x)=P_{r_i^2}(\chi G)(x)+\lambda r_i^2\mathrm{Id},
 \qquad H_i(x)=Q_i(x)^{-1}.$$ Then $$\begin{aligned}
 &(1-o(1))R_i(x)\le Q_i(x)\le(1+o(1))R_i(x),
       \qquad Q_0(x)=\mathrm{Id}+o(1),\label{co:QR-comparison}\\
 &H_i(x)\le H_{i+1}(x)\le(1+o(1))H_i(x).
       \label{co:H-monotone}
\end{aligned}$$ The nearby-center and slow-growth estimates of Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a> hold for $Q_i$ as well. In particular, for every prescribed $a>0$, with a sufficiently late rescaling, $$\label{co:slow-QH}
 Q_j(x)\le C_a2^{2a(i-j)}Q_i(x)\quad(j\le i),
 \qquad \|H_i(x)\|\le C_a2^{2ai}.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-heat-matrices"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-heat-matrices</summary>

<a id="co:raw-mass-growth"></a>
*Proof.* The original gradient bound and a Bochner cutoff on a ball of radius $s\ge1$ give, for centers in a fixed bounded set and up to the support region, $$\label{co:raw-mass-growth}
 M(B_s(x))[v]\le C(s^{n-2}+\kappa s^n)|v|^2
 \le Cs^{n-2}|v|^2.$$ For radii larger than the support radius the same bound holds with $\chi M$ in place of $M$, by monotonicity and $n\ge2$. The cutoff for this estimate lies inside the expanding domain where <a href="#co:original-gradient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:original-gradient-bound">[co:original-gradient-bound]</a> holds. The same estimate for the Hessian, followed by Cauchy–Schwarz in the product rule, gives $$\int|E_{\rm cut}|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le CT^{n-2}.$$ Since its support is a distance at least $T/2$ from the fixed testing centers for large $\nu$, <a href="#co:kernel-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:kernel-bounds">[co:kernel-bounds]</a> yields <a id="co:cutoff-small"></a>$$\label{co:cutoff-small}
 \sup_{0<t\le1}\|P_t E_{\rm cut}(x)\|
 \le C T^{n-2}\sup_{0<t\le1}t^{-n/2}e^{-cT^2/t}
 =o(1).$$ Choose $\lambda$ at least this supremum plus $2\kappa\|G\|_\infty$, and add a positive number tending to zero. Equations <a href="#co:semigroup-measure" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:semigroup-measure">[co:semigroup-measure]</a> and <a href="#co:cutoff-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cutoff-source">[co:cutoff-source]</a> give $$\partial_t(P_t(\chi G)+\lambda t\mathrm{Id})
 =2P_t(\chi M)-2\kappa P_t(\chi G)
     +P_tE_{\rm cut}+\lambda\mathrm{Id}.$$ The last three terms have nonnegative sum and bounded norm. This proves <a href="#co:heat-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:heat-derivative">[co:heat-derivative]</a>, in fact with lower bound $2P_t(\chi M)$.

We verify relative, rather than merely absolute, comparison with the ball averages. Fix $i,x$ and conjugate by $R_i(x)^{-1/2}$. On every fixed ball $B_{Lr_i}(x)$, the conjugated Gram field converges in normalized $L^1$ to $\mathrm{Id}$, by Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a>. The heat kernel at time $r_i^2$ is at most $Cr_i^{-n}$, so the integral of its local error tends to zero. The tails are uniform in $i$: on the shell with radius $2^m r_i\le1$, slow growth bounds the conjugated Gram average by $C2^{2am}$, while the Gaussian bounds its contribution by $$C2^{(n+2a)m}e^{-c4^m}.$$ When $2^m r_i>1$, use the original gradient bound and <a href="#co:R-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:R-lower">[co:R-lower]</a>; its conjugation cost $Cr_i^{-2a}$ is at most $C2^{2am}$. Volume comparison and the same Gaussian again give the displayed summable bound. This treats the entire support of $\chi$. The missing mass from $1-\chi$ is bounded in the same way, using total heat mass one. First choose $L$ large enough to make the tail small and then choose the rescaling late enough to make the local error small. Beyond the expanding almost Euclidean region, the global volume bound adds at most $e^{C2^m r_i}$ to a shell estimate; since $r_i\le1$, this factor is absorbed by decreasing the Gaussian constant in $e^{-c4^m}$. Thus the assertion about the missing heat mass is uniform there as well. Finally, $$\lambda r_i^2R_i(x)^{-1}\le C_a\lambda r_i^{2-2a}\mathrm{Id}=o(1)\mathrm{Id}$$ for $a<1$. This proves <a href="#co:QR-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:QR-comparison">[co:QR-comparison]</a>; its initial scale assertion follows from Euclidean convergence.

The derivative positivity makes $Q_i\ge Q_{i+1}$, hence $H_i\le H_{i+1}$. Relative comparison with $R_i$ and <a href="#co:nearby-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:nearby-R">[co:nearby-R]</a> give the adjacent-scale upper bound and all fixed nearby comparisons. Iteration gives <a href="#co:slow-QH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-QH">[co:slow-QH]</a>, choosing the adjacent-scale tolerance after the prescribed exponent $a$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The dyadic heat comparison

<a id="co:N-small"></a>
The matrices <a id="co:N-definition"></a>$$\label{co:N-definition}
 N_j(x)=r_j^{2-n}M(B_{r_j}(x))$$ are masses in the original harmonic coordinate components. They have not been conjugated by a Gram inverse. In particular, Lemma <a href="#co:bochner-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:bochner-small">6</a> and <a href="#co:QR-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:QR-comparison">[co:QR-comparison]</a> give $$\label{co:N-small}
 N_j(x)\le\varepsilon_\nu Q_j(x),
 \qquad \varepsilon_\nu\longrightarrow0,$$ uniformly in $j$ and in fixed testing regions. We allow $\varepsilon_\nu$ to increase by fixed multiplicative constants in what follows.

<a id="stmt-co-heat-comparison"></a>
<div id="co:heat-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="co:heat-decrement"></a>
<a id="co:bilinear-heat-comparison"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Dyadic heat comparison). *Fix constants $A,C_0\ge1$, where $C_0$ bounds $d(x,y)/r_i$, and fix a bounded region for $x$. There is a fixed exponent $\gamma>0$, which may be taken to be $1/2$, with the following properties for all sufficiently late rescalings. Set <a id="co:F-definition"></a>$$\label{co:F-definition}
 \mathcal F_i(x)=2^{-\gamma i}\mathrm{Id}+
       \sum_{j=0}^i2^{-\gamma(i-j)}N_j(x).$$ For the prescribed enlargement $A$, $$\label{co:heat-decrement}
 c_A r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Ar_i}(x))
 \le Q_i(x)-Q_{i+1}(x)\le C\mathcal F_i(x).$$ For $d(x,y)\le C_0r_i$ and $v,w\in\mathbb R^n$, $$\label{co:bilinear-heat-comparison}
 |v^T(R_i(y)-Q_i(x))w|
 \le C|v|_{Q_i(x)}\sqrt{\mathcal F_i(x)[w]}.$$ All constants are independent of $i$ and of the expanding cutoff radius. They may depend on the stated fixed parameters.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-heat-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-heat-comparison</summary>

<a id="co:raw-bilinear-heat"></a>
*Proof.* *The semigroup identity.* Write $r=r_i$, suppress $x$ in $N_j,Q_j$, and let $\mathcal A f=\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_r(y)}f\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m$. We first prove the more explicit estimate $$\label{co:raw-bilinear-heat}
 \begin{split}
 |v^T(R_i(y)-Q_i(x))w|
 &\le Cr|v||w|\\
 &\quad+C\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(i-j)}
                   \sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}.
 \end{split}$$ This also specifies all the spatial and time tails entering <a href="#co:bilinear-heat-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:bilinear-heat-comparison">[co:bilinear-heat-comparison]</a>.

<a id="co:average-heat-identity"></a>
Put $f=\chi G$, $S=\Delta f$, and use the kernel functions from Lemma <a href="#co:kernel-measures" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:kernel-measures">8</a>. Since $\chi=1$ on the averaging ball, the exact semigroup identity is $$\label{co:average-heat-identity}
 \begin{split}
 R_i(y)-Q_i(x)
 ={}&\mathcal A P_1f-P_1f(x)-\lambda r^2\mathrm{Id}\\
 &-\int_0^{r^2}\mathcal A P_tS\,\mathrm dt
  -\int_{r^2}^1\bigl(\mathcal A P_tS-P_tS(x)\bigr)\,\mathrm dt.
 \end{split}$$ The averaged endpoint at zero is justified in Lemma <a href="#co:kernel-measures" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:kernel-measures">8</a>; it does not require pointwise convergence of the Gram field.

<a id="co:integrated-short-kernel"></a>
*The local source.* Consider the contribution of $2\chi M$ on $B_1(x)$. The integrated short-time kernel obeys $$\label{co:integrated-short-kernel}
 \int_0^{r^2}K_t(z)\,\mathrm dt
 \le Cr^{2-n}e^{-c d(x,z)^2/r^2}.$$ For $0\le q<i$, integration of <a href="#co:long-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:long-kernel">[co:long-kernel]</a> on the time block $I_q=[r_{q+1}^2,r_q^2]$ gives <a id="co:integrated-long-kernel"></a>$$\label{co:integrated-long-kernel}
 \int_{I_q}|K_t(z)-p_t(x,z)|\,\mathrm dt
 \le Cr r_q^{1-n}e^{-c d(x,z)^2/r_q^2}.$$ To integrate a Gaussian at scale $r_q$ against $M(v,w)$, partition $B_1(x)$ into $B_{r_q}(x)$ and the annuli $$B_{r_j}(x)\setminus B_{r_{j+1}}(x),\qquad 0\le j<q.$$ Define positive numerical coefficients <a id="co:gaussian-shell-coefficients"></a>$$\label{co:gaussian-shell-coefficients}
 k_0=1,\qquad
 k_h=2^{(n-2)h}e^{-c4^{h-1}}\quad(h\ge1).
 \qquad \sum_{h=0}^\infty2^h k_h<\infty.$$ On the annulus indexed by $j<q$, the distance from $x$ is at least $2^{q-j-1}r_q$. Equation <a href="#co:matrix-measure-cs" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:matrix-measure-cs">[co:matrix-measure-cs]</a> and the identity $M(B_{r_j}(x))=r_j^{n-2}N_j$ show that the contribution of the time block $I_q$ is at most $$C2^{-(i-q)}\sum_{j=0}^q k_{q-j}
                       \sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}.$$ Reversing the finite sums, the sum of all these contributions is at most $$\begin{aligned}
 &C\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}2^{-(i-j)}
       \left(\sum_{h=0}^{i-1-j}2^h k_h\right)
                       \sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}\\
 &\hspace{25mm}\le
 C\sum_{j=0}^{i-1}2^{-(i-j)}\sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}.
\end{aligned}$$ The short-time term uses the same partition with $q=i$ and <a href="#co:integrated-short-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-short-kernel">[co:integrated-short-kernel]</a>. It is bounded by $$C\sum_{j=0}^i k_{i-j}\sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}
 \le C\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(i-j)}\sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}.$$ This proves the required contribution on $B_1(x)$, with the original unit-ball $N_j$ and no unmentioned enlarged mass.

<a id="co:exterior-integrated-kernel"></a>
*The remaining source terms.* We treat all remaining parts of <a href="#co:average-heat-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:average-heat-identity">[co:average-heat-identity]</a>. If $D=d(x,z)\ge1$, the integrated kernel bounds imply $$\label{co:exterior-integrated-kernel}
 \int_0^{r^2}K_t(z)\,\mathrm dt+
 \int_{r^2}^1|K_t(z)-p_t(x,z)|\,\mathrm dt
 \le Cr e^{-c'D^2}.$$ For the second term, split the Gaussian into two factors, use one to retain $e^{-c'D^2}$, and use the other to integrate $t^{-(n+1)/2}e^{-c''/t}$ on $(0,1)$. For the first term, <a href="#co:integrated-short-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:integrated-short-kernel">[co:integrated-short-kernel]</a> reduces the assertion to absorbing $r^{1-n}$ in $e^{-c''/r^2}$. Both bounds are uniform for $0<r\le1$.

Equation <a href="#co:raw-mass-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-mass-growth">[co:raw-mass-growth]</a>, summed over the annuli $B_{2^{h+1}}(x)\setminus B_{2^h}(x)$, gives $$\int e^{-c'd(x,z)^2}\,\mathrm d(\chi M[v])(z)\le C|v|^2.$$ Therefore <a href="#co:exterior-integrated-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:exterior-integrated-kernel">[co:exterior-integrated-kernel]</a> and polarized Cauchy–Schwarz bound the exterior positive-source contribution by $Cr|v||w|$. The total variation of the cutoff source is $O(T^{n-2})$, is supported a distance comparable with $T$ from $x$, and has the same Gaussian moment bound, in fact a bound tending to zero. Its contribution is also at most $Cr|v||w|$.

The curvature source in <a href="#co:cutoff-source" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cutoff-source">[co:cutoff-source]</a> has bounded density, of norm at most $C\kappa$. For its short-time part use heat mass one. For its long-time part, integration of <a href="#co:long-kernel" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:long-kernel">[co:long-kernel]</a> in the source variable gives $Cr/\sqrt t$. This last Gaussian integral follows from local volume comparison for nearby shells and the global exponential volume bound for remote shells. Consequently the curvature contribution is at most $$C\kappa\left(r^2+\int_{r^2}^1 r t^{-1/2}\,\mathrm dt\right)|v||w|
 \le Cr|v||w|.$$ The time-one heat regularization of the bounded matrix field $f$ has uniformly bounded spatial gradient in the testing region, by the integrated first kernel gradient estimate. Hence $|v^T(\mathcal A P_1f-P_1f(x))w|\le Cr|v||w|$. Also $\lambda r^2|v||w|\le Cr|v||w|$. These facts prove <a href="#co:raw-bilinear-heat" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-bilinear-heat">[co:raw-bilinear-heat]</a>.

*Normalization and decrements.* Choose any sufficiently small fixed growth exponent $a>0$ in <a href="#co:slow-QH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-QH">[co:slow-QH]</a>. By <a href="#co:N-small" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:N-small">[co:N-small]</a>, for $j\le i$, $$\sqrt{N_j[v]}\le C2^{a(i-j)}|v|_{Q_i}.$$ Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz now gives, for instance when $a<1/8$, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(i-j)}\sqrt{N_j[v]N_j[w]}
 &\le C|v|_{Q_i}\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(1-a)(i-j)}\sqrt{N_j[w]}\\
 &\le C|v|_{Q_i}
       \left(\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(i-j)/2}N_j[w]\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}$$ Indeed the unused scalar series has terms $2^{-(2(1-a)-1/2)(i-j)}$, whose sum is bounded. Since $Q_i\ge c_a r_i^{2a}\mathrm{Id}$, the raw term in <a href="#co:raw-bilinear-heat" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-bilinear-heat">[co:raw-bilinear-heat]</a> is at most $$Cr_i^{1-a}|v|_{Q_i}|w|
 \le C|v|_{Q_i}\bigl(2^{-i/2}|w|^2\bigr)^{1/2}.$$ This proves <a href="#co:bilinear-heat-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:bilinear-heat-comparison">[co:bilinear-heat-comparison]</a> with $\gamma=1/2$.

It remains to prove the decrement estimates. Integrate <a href="#co:heat-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:heat-derivative">[co:heat-derivative]</a> on $[r_i^2/4,r_i^2]$. On a fixed ball $B_{Ar_i}(x)$, where $\chi=1$, the heat lower bound <a href="#co:kernel-lower" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:kernel-lower">[co:kernel-lower]</a> gives the left inequality in <a href="#co:heat-decrement" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:heat-decrement">[co:heat-decrement]</a>. The upper bound is $$Q_i-Q_{i+1}
 \le Cr_i^{2-n}\int e^{-c d(x,z)^2/r_i^2}\,\mathrm d(\chi M)(z)
        +Cr_i^2\mathrm{Id}.$$ On $B_1(x)$, the shell computation with $q=i$ bounds the positive term by $C\sum_{j=0}^i k_{i-j}N_j$, which is at most $C\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-(i-j)/2}N_j$. Outside $B_1(x)$, <a href="#co:raw-mass-growth" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:raw-mass-growth">[co:raw-mass-growth]</a> and Gaussian decay give $Cr_i^2\mathrm{Id}$, after absorbing every fixed power of $r_i^{-1}$ in $e^{-c/r_i^2}$. Since $r_i^2\le2^{-i/2}$, this proves the upper bound with the same $\gamma$. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-enlargement-indexing"></a>
<div id="co:enlargement-indexing" class="remark">

*Remark 11* (Fixed enlargements and indexing). No enlarged mass was needed in the preceding bilinear estimate. When one is used elsewhere, it can be returned to the same notation without losing the decay. Indeed, for fixed $A\ge1$ put $h=\lceil\log_2 A\rceil$. For $j\ge h$, $$r_j^{2-n}M(B_{Ar_j}(x))\le2^{h(n-2)}N_{j-h}(x).$$ The finitely many $j<h$ are bounded by $C_A\mathrm{Id}$ in the original coordinate components, by a fixed-scale cutoff estimate. Thus an exponentially weighted sum of these enlarged masses is bounded by a fixed multiple of $2^{-\gamma i}\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{j\le i}2^{-\gamma(i-j)}N_j$. A center shift bounded by $C_0r_j$ only increases $A$ by $C_0$. In contrast, the mass matrices $D_{i,y}$ below include two Gram inverses; their comparison requires the argument that follows.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Accumulation without commuting matrices

We separate the finite-dimensional calculation from its geometric application. All inequalities in this subsection are inequalities of quadratic forms on the fixed space $\mathbb R^n$.

<a id="stmt-co-inverse-increments"></a>
<div id="co:inverse-increments" class="lemma">

<a id="co:inverse-increment-order"></a>
<a id="co:weighted-total"></a>
<a id="co:central-cumulative"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Weighted inverse increments). *Fix a testing center $x$, and suppress it in the notation. Let $a>0$ in <a href="#co:slow-QH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-QH">[co:slow-QH]</a> be chosen so that $4a<\gamma$. Set $$B_i=H_{i+1}-H_i,\qquad \Delta_i=Q_i-Q_{i+1},
 \qquad a_i=H_i\xi$$ for a fixed $\xi\in\mathbb R^n$. Then, uniformly for every integer $k\ge0$, $$\begin{aligned}
 &H_i\Delta_iH_i\le B_i\le(1+C\varepsilon_\nu)H_i\Delta_iH_i,
             \label{co:inverse-increment-order}\\
 &\sum_{i<k}\mathcal F_i[a_i]\le C H_k[\xi],
             \label{co:weighted-total}\\
 &C^{-1}H_k\le \mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i<k}H_iN_iH_i\le C H_k.
             \label{co:central-cumulative}
\end{aligned}$$ For every fixed $A\ge1$, the upper sum bound also holds with $r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Ar_i}(x))$ in place of $N_i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-inverse-increments"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-inverse-increments</summary>

*Proof.* Let $E_i=Q_i^{-1/2}\Delta_iQ_i^{-1/2}$. By <a href="#co:H-monotone" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:H-monotone">[co:H-monotone]</a>, $0\le E_i\le\varepsilon_\nu\mathrm{Id}$ after increasing the sequence $\varepsilon_\nu\to0$. The exact inverse identity is $$B_i=Q_i^{-1/2}\bigl((\mathrm{Id}-E_i)^{-1}-\mathrm{Id}\bigr)Q_i^{-1/2}.$$ The spectral inequality $E_i\le(\mathrm{Id}-E_i)^{-1}-\mathrm{Id}\le(1+C\varepsilon_\nu)E_i$ proves <a href="#co:inverse-increment-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:inverse-increment-order">[co:inverse-increment-order]</a>. This use of the functional calculus is for one conjugated positive matrix at a time; matrices from different scales need not commute.

<a id="co:convolution"></a>
<a id="co:background-sum"></a>
We spell out the convolution estimate. Since $0\le B_l\le C\varepsilon_\nu H_l$, conjugation by $Q_l^{1/2}$ gives $B_lQ_lB_l\le C B_l$. Consequently, by <a href="#co:slow-QH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-QH">[co:slow-QH]</a>, <a id="co:B-vector-bound"></a>$$\label{co:B-vector-bound}
 |B_l\xi|_{Q_j}^2\le C2^{2a(l-j)}B_l[\xi],\qquad j\le l.$$ For $j<i$, set $m=i-j$. The identity $a_i-a_j=\sum_{l=j}^{i-1}B_l\xi$ and Cauchy–Schwarz give $$|a_i-a_j|_{Q_j}^2
 \le C m2^{2am}\sum_{l=j}^{i-1}B_l[\xi].$$ Using $N_j\le\varepsilon_\nu Q_j$, it follows that $$\begin{aligned}
 &\sum_{i<k}\sum_{j\le i}2^{-\gamma(i-j)}N_j[a_i]
       \label{co:convolution}\\[-2mm]
 &\qquad\le C\sum_{j<k}N_j[a_j]
       +C\varepsilon_\nu
          \sum_{i<k}\sum_{j<i}(i-j)2^{-(\gamma-2a)(i-j)}
                    \sum_{l=j}^{i-1}B_l[\xi]\notag\\
 &\qquad\le C\sum_{j<k}N_j[a_j]
             +C\varepsilon_\nu H_k[\xi].\notag
\end{aligned}$$ For the last inequality, fix $l$. For a specified $m=i-j$, there are at most $m$ intervals $[j,i)$ of length $m$ containing $l$. Hence its coefficient is bounded by $C\sum_{m\ge1}m^2 2^{-(\gamma-2a)m}<\infty$. Also $\sum_{l<k}B_l[\xi]=(H_k-H_0)[\xi]\le H_k[\xi]$. The identity contribution is bounded separately using <a href="#co:slow-QH" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-QH">[co:slow-QH]</a>: $$\label{co:background-sum}
 \sum_{i<k}2^{-\gamma i}|a_i|^2
 \le C|\xi|^2\sum_{i\ge0}2^{-(\gamma-4a)i}
 \le C|\xi|^2.$$

The lower heat decrement with $A=1$, together with <a href="#co:inverse-increment-order" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:inverse-increment-order">[co:inverse-increment-order]</a>, gives $$\sum_{i<k}N_i[a_i]\le C\sum_{i<k}B_i[\xi]
 \le C H_k[\xi].$$ Conversely, the upper heat decrement yields $$H_k[\xi]\le C|\xi|^2+C\sum_{i<k}\mathcal F_i[a_i]
 \le C|\xi|^2+C\sum_{i<k}N_i[a_i]
                         +C\varepsilon_\nu H_k[\xi].$$ Choose the rescaling late enough to absorb the last term. This proves <a href="#co:central-cumulative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:central-cumulative">[co:central-cumulative]</a>, since $H_0$ is comparable with $\mathrm{Id}$. Equations <a href="#co:convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:convolution">[co:convolution]</a> and <a href="#co:background-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:background-sum">[co:background-sum]</a> now imply <a href="#co:weighted-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:weighted-total">[co:weighted-total]</a>. Finally the lower heat decrement for a general fixed $A$ bounds each enlarged-mass quadratic form on $a_i$ by a fixed multiple of $B_i[\xi]$. Summing proves the last assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-normalization-error"></a>
<div id="co:normalization-error" class="lemma">

<a id="co:normalization-error-pointwise"></a>
<a id="co:normalization-error-sum"></a>
<a id="co:normalization-mass-error"></a>
**Lemma 13** (Changing the mean normalization). *Fix $C_0\ge1$. For $d(x,y)\le C_0r_i$, put $$A_{i,y}=R_i(y)-Q_i(x),\qquad
 e_{i,y}=R_i(y)^{-1}\xi-H_i(x)\xi.$$ Then $$\label{co:normalization-error-pointwise}
 |e_{i,y}|_{Q_i(x)}^2
 \le C\mathcal F_i(x)[H_i(x)\xi].$$ If at each $i<k$ at most $J$ such centers are chosen, then $$\label{co:normalization-error-sum}
 \sum_{i<k}\sum_y|e_{i,y}|_{Q_i(x)}^2
 \le C_J H_k(x)[\xi].$$ For any positive semidefinite matrices $C_{i,y}$ satisfying $C_{i,y}\le\varepsilon_\nu Q_i(x)$, their error cost is $$\label{co:normalization-mass-error}
 \sum_{i<k}\sum_y C_{i,y}[e_{i,y}]
 \le C_J\varepsilon_\nu H_k(x)[\xi].$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-normalization-error"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-normalization-error</summary>

<a id="co:bilinear-dual-form"></a>
*Proof.* Taking the supremum over $v$ with $|v|_{Q_i}=1$ in <a href="#co:bilinear-heat-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:bilinear-heat-comparison">[co:bilinear-heat-comparison]</a> gives $$\label{co:bilinear-dual-form}
 A_{i,y}H_iA_{i,y}\le C\mathcal F_i.$$ This follows directly from $\sup_{|v|_{Q_i}=1}|v^Tz|^2=z^TH_i z$. Write $a_i=H_i\xi$. The inverse identity is $$e_{i,y}=-R_i(y)^{-1}A_{i,y}a_i.$$ Relative comparison $R_i(y)\asymp Q_i(x)$ implies $R_i(y)^{-1}Q_i(x)R_i(y)^{-1}\le C H_i(x)$. Therefore <a href="#co:bilinear-dual-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:bilinear-dual-form">[co:bilinear-dual-form]</a> gives $$|e_{i,y}|_{Q_i}^2
 \le C a_i^T A_{i,y}H_iA_{i,y}a_i
 \le C\mathcal F_i[a_i].$$ This proves <a href="#co:normalization-error-pointwise" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:normalization-error-pointwise">[co:normalization-error-pointwise]</a>. Sum it, use the bound $J$, and apply <a href="#co:weighted-total" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:weighted-total">[co:weighted-total]</a> to obtain <a href="#co:normalization-error-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:normalization-error-sum">[co:normalization-error-sum]</a>. The last assertion follows by $C_{i,y}[e_{i,y}]\le\varepsilon_\nu|e_{i,y}|_{Q_i}^2$. No commutation of $R_i(y)$ with $Q_i(x)$ has been used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-co-cumulative-comparison"></a>
<div id="co:cumulative-comparison" class="lemma">

<a id="co:cumulative-estimates"></a>
**Lemma 14** (Cumulative comparison for local mass matrices). *Fix constants $A_0,C_0,L\ge1$ and a positive integer $J$. At each center $x\in B_{A_0}(p)$ and each scale $i\ge0$, choose at most $J$ centers $y$ with $d(x,y)\le C_0r_i$. The choice may depend arbitrarily on $x,i$. Set <a id="co:D-definition"></a>$$\label{co:D-definition}
 C_{i,y}=r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Lr_i}(y)),
 \qquad D_{i,y}=R_i(y)^{-1}C_{i,y}R_i(y)^{-1}.$$ Give the chosen centers weights $\theta_{i,y}\in[0,1]$, and suppose that for every $i$ at least one has weight one and satisfies $B_{Lr_i}(y)\supset B_{r_i}(x)$. Write <a id="co:ZS-definition"></a>$$\label{co:ZS-definition}
 Z_i=\sum_yD_{i,y},\qquad
 S_i=\sum_y\theta_{i,y}D_{i,y}.$$ There is $C_*<\infty$, depending only on $n,A_0,C_0,L,J$, such that for all sufficiently late rescalings, every such choice, and every integer $k\ge0$, $$\label{co:cumulative-estimates}
 Z_i\le o(1)H_i(x),\qquad
 \mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i<k}Z_i\le C_*H_k(x),\qquad
 H_k(x)\le C_*\left(\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i<k}S_i\right).$$ The $o(1)$ is uniform over the stated centers, all scales, and all choices satisfying these conditions.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-co-cumulative-comparison"><summary>Proof of stmt-co-cumulative-comparison</summary>

*Proof.* Fix $x$ and $\xi$, and suppress $x$ from the notation. By <a href="#co:small-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:small-mass">[co:small-mass]</a> and nearby Gram comparison, $C_{i,y}\le\varepsilon_\nu Q_i$, uniformly in all the choices. Conjugation by $R_i(y)^{-1}$, followed by relative comparison, gives $D_{i,y}\le C\varepsilon_\nu H_i$. Bounded overlap proves the first assertion of <a href="#co:cumulative-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cumulative-estimates">[co:cumulative-estimates]</a>.

For the upper sum, use $R_i(y)^{-1}\xi=a_i+e_{i,y}$. Positivity gives $$D_{i,y}[\xi]=C_{i,y}[a_i+e_{i,y}]
 \le2C_{i,y}[a_i]+2C_{i,y}[e_{i,y}].$$ The ball defining $C_{i,y}$ is contained in $B_{(L+C_0)r_i}(x)$. The enlarged upper sum assertion of Lemma <a href="#co:inverse-increments" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:inverse-increments">12</a> therefore bounds the sum of its first terms by $C_J H_k[\xi]$. Equation <a href="#co:normalization-mass-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:normalization-mass-error">[co:normalization-mass-error]</a> bounds the sum of its second terms by $C_J\varepsilon_\nu H_k[\xi]$. Since $\mathrm{Id}\le C H_0\le C H_k$, this proves the middle assertion in <a href="#co:cumulative-estimates" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:cumulative-estimates">[co:cumulative-estimates]</a>.

For the reverse direction, at each scale select one of the weight-one centers whose ball covers $B_{r_i}(x)$. Then $N_i\le C_{i,y}$. Write $a_i=R_i(y)^{-1}\xi-e_{i,y}$ to obtain $$N_i[a_i]\le2C_{i,y}[R_i(y)^{-1}\xi]
                +2C_{i,y}[e_{i,y}]
 \le2S_i[\xi]+2C_{i,y}[e_{i,y}].$$ The central comparison <a href="#co:central-cumulative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:central-cumulative">[co:central-cumulative]</a> and the normalization-error bound give $$H_k[\xi]\le C\left(|\xi|^2+\sum_{i<k}S_i[\xi]\right)
                      +C\varepsilon_\nu H_k[\xi].$$ Choose the rescaling sufficiently late after all fixed constants and absorb the last term. The result is uniform in $k,x,\xi$ and the center choices. Taking all vectors $\xi$ proves the last matrix inequality and completes the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pi:section"></a>
### Summable directional pinching

We prove the estimate which relates a difference of coordinate values to the Bochner measure. The summable quantity in this section is an energy; its square root will occur in individual estimates, but will never be summed without an exponentially decreasing weight.

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### Conventions and domains

We use the sequence of rescaled spaces and harmonic columns constructed in Section <a href="#co:section" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:section">2</a>. We suppress the index of that sequence. Thus the lower Ricci bound is $-\kappa$, with $\kappa\to0$, and $o(1)$ is uniform over the centers and dyadic scales under consideration. The measure is always the Hausdorff measure in the current length units. We write $r_i=2^{-i}$, $i\ge0$, and use the matrices $R_i$ and the matrix-valued positive measure $M(u)$ from the preceding section.

<a id="pi:center-configuration"></a>
<a id="pi:radii"></a>
Fix numbers $L,C_{\mathrm{sep}}>1$. The final estimate will involve the mass on $B_{Lr_i}(y)$ and centers satisfying $$x,z,y\in B_4(p),\qquad
 d(x,y),d(z,y)\le C_{\mathrm{sep}}r_i.
 \label{pi:center-configuration}$$ There is no restriction in replacing an initially prescribed number by a larger number greater than one. Set $$R=16(L+C_{\mathrm{sep}}+2),\qquad S=32R.
 \label{pi:radii}$$ All the balls used below are contained in $B_{16S+4}(p)$ before the additional dyadic rescaling. We choose this fixed enlargement first and then take a sufficiently late member of the sequence. In particular, the harmonic functions are defined there and all the conclusions of Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a> apply on its fixed enlargements. Poisson solutions centered at $a$ will be defined on $B_{8Sr_i}(a)$. Their estimates will be used on $B_{2Sr_i}(a)$; the affine fits will be made on $B_{Sr_i}(a)$; radial invariance will be used only on $B_{Sr_i/2}(a)$. In the two-center argument, its projection ball is $B_{Rr_i}(z)$ and the transport calculation uses $B_{3Rr_i}(z)$. These choices leave strict margins between every testing ball and its domain of definition.

For a measurable ball $B$ of positive measure, put $$\|f\|_{2,B}^{\mathrm{av}}
 =\left(\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_B |f|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right)^{1/2}.$$ Unadorned $L^2$ norms below use integrals, rather than averages. After a dyadic rescaling, the volumes of all fixed-radius balls in use are bounded above and below by positive constants. Thus these two norm conventions differ by uniformly bounded factors at each of the fixed radii.

<a id="pi:dirichlet-sobolev"></a>
<a id="pi:coordinate-input"></a>
We use two consequences of the preceding section. First, the Dirichlet inequalities on the rescaled balls in use include, for some $d>1$ and a constant depending on the fixed radius, $$\|\psi\|_{L^{2d}}^2\le C\int |\nabla\psi|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m,
 \qquad \psi\in W^{1,2}_0(B).
 \label{pi:dirichlet-sobolev}$$ as provided by Lemma <a href="#co:local-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:local-calculus">3</a>, specifically <a href="#co:dirichlet-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:dirichlet-sobolev">[co:dirichlet-sobolev]</a>. The corresponding Poincaré inequality is included. In dimension two one may choose any one of the available exponents strictly greater than two. The domains are proper subsets of larger balls whose complements have a fixed positive volume, so the Dirichlet inequality is coercive. Second, if $$\sigma_i^a(b)=|b|_{R_i(a)},\qquad b\in\mathbb R^n,$$ then, after fixing for example $a_0=1/16$ in the growth estimate, $$\frac{\sigma_j^a(b)}{\sigma_i^a(b)}
 \le C2^{a_0|i-j|}\quad(b\ne0),\qquad
 \sup_{B_{2Sr_i}(a)}|\nabla(b\cdot u)|
 \le C\sigma_i^a(b).
 \label{pi:coordinate-input}$$ Each constant in this section may depend on $n,L,C_{\mathrm{sep}}$, and consequently on $R,S$, but not on $i$, the centers, or the late member of the sequence.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Positive sources and Poisson pinching

Poisson replacements of squared distance follow the almost-rigidity method of Cheeger and Colding (Cheeger and Colding 1996) <a id="pm-cite-41"></a>[[CC96](#ref-CC96)]; see also the quantitative regularization in (Cheeger, Jiang, and Naber 2021, sec. 6.1) <a id="pm-cite-42"></a>[[CJN21](#ref-CJN21)]. We retain the full positive Bochner measure and prove the uniform estimates needed at all testing centers.
