The following elementary Dirichlet-form observation is useful because an application of Cauchy–Schwarz to the energy estimate alone would not give the required dependence on the mass.

<a id="stmt-pi-positive-source"></a>
<div id="pi:positive-source" class="lemma">

**Lemma 15** (A linear gradient bound for a positive source). *Let $\Omega$ be one of the bounded rescaled domains satisfying <a href="#pi:dirichlet-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:dirichlet-sobolev">[pi:dirichlet-sobolev]</a>, and suppose that $h\in W^{1,2}_0(\Omega)$ is nonnegative and bounded, with $$\int\langle\nabla h,\nabla\psi\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 =\int\psi\,\mathrm d\mu$$ for compactly supported Lipschitz tests, where $\mu$ is a finite positive measure. If $\mu(\Omega)\le P$, then <a id="pi:positive-source-bounds"></a>$$\int_\Omega|\nabla h|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le CP,
 \qquad
 \int_\Omega|\nabla h|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le\|h\|_\infty P.
 \label{pi:positive-source-bounds}$$ The constant in the first inequality depends only on the Sobolev constant, its exponent, and an upper bound for $\mathfrak m(\Omega)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-positive-source"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-positive-source</summary>

*Proof.* We first explain the interpretation of tests against $\mu$. Since $\mu=-\Delta h$, its action extends continuously to $W^{1,2}_0(\Omega)$. This positive functional does not charge a set of zero Sobolev capacity: for a compact such set, test it with nonnegative functions which are at least one near that set and whose $W^{1,2}_0$ norms tend to zero, and use continuity of the functional. Regularity of the measure gives the same conclusion for a Borel set of zero capacity. Consequently bounded Sobolev functions are integrated using their quasi-continuous representatives. Approximation in $W^{1,2}_0$, followed by bounded truncation and a subsequence converging outside a set of zero capacity, shows that the functional and the measure integral agree on these tests. In particular all bounded Lipschitz compositions of $h$ which vanish at zero are admissible. This justifies the truncations used next without making a choice of arbitrary $\mathfrak m$-almost-everywhere representatives on the support of $\mu$.

<a id="pi:source-moment"></a>
If $P=0$, testing with $h$ proves $h=0$. Suppose $P>0$ and put $H=h/P$. For $t>0$, the test $\min\{H,t\}$ gives $$\int_{\{H<t\}}|\nabla H|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le t.$$ Applying <a href="#pi:dirichlet-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:dirichlet-sobolev">[pi:dirichlet-sobolev]</a> to the same truncation yields $$t^2\mathfrak m(H\ge t)^{1/d}\le Ct,
 \qquad \mathfrak m(H\ge t)\le Ct^{-d}.$$ Choose $0<\alpha<d-1$. The layer-cake formula, using the volume bound for $t\le1$ and the last estimate for $t\ge1$, implies $$\int_\Omega(1+H)^{1+\alpha}\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le C.
 \label{pi:source-moment}$$ The bounded test $1-(1+H)^{-\alpha}$ gives $$\alpha\int_\Omega
 \frac{|\nabla H|^2}{(1+H)^{1+\alpha}}\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\le1.$$ Combining this inequality with <a href="#pi:source-moment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:source-moment">[pi:source-moment]</a> by weighted Cauchy–Schwarz proves $\int|\nabla H|\le C$, which is the first assertion. Finally $h$ itself is an admissible bounded test, so $\int|\nabla h|^2=\int h\,\mathrm d\mu\le\|h\|_\infty P$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pi:shifted-triangle"></a>
For a function with constant Laplacian and a scalar $b$, write <a id="pi:shifted-measure"></a>$$\mathcal B_\kappa(w,b)
 =M(w)-(2b\Delta w-nb^2)\mathfrak m.
 \label{pi:shifted-measure}$$ By Lemma <a href="#co:local-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:local-calculus">3</a>, this is a nonnegative measure and <a id="pi:shifted-lower-bound"></a>$$\mathcal B_\kappa(w,b)
 \ge |\operatorname{Hess}w-bg|^2\mathfrak m.
 \label{pi:shifted-lower-bound}$$ It is quadratic in the pair $(w,b)$. Positivity, applied to the sum and difference of two such pairs, gives the measure inequality $$\mathcal B_\kappa(w_1-w_2,b_1-b_2)
 \le2\mathcal B_\kappa(w_1,b_1)
    +2\mathcal B_\kappa(w_2,b_2).
 \label{pi:shifted-triangle}$$ These statements use the noncollapsed dimension and trace identity and the improved Bochner inequality; see (De Philippis and Gigli 2018; Han 2018; Gigli 2018) <a id="pm-cite-43"></a>[[DPG18](#ref-DPG18), [Han18](#ref-Han18), [Gigli18](#ref-Gigli18)].

<a id="stmt-pi-poisson-pinching"></a>
<div id="pi:poisson-pinching" class="lemma">

<a id="pi:poisson-sum"></a>
<a id="pi:poisson-definition"></a>
<a id="pi:poisson-boundedness"></a>
<a id="pi:poisson-errors"></a>
<a id="pi:poisson-shifted-mass"></a>
**Lemma 16** (Poisson replacements with summable defects). *There are nonnegative numbers $P_i(a)$, defined for every $a\in B_4(p)$ and $i\ge0$, with $$\sup_{a\in B_4(p)}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}P_i(a)=o(1),
 \label{pi:poisson-sum}$$ and functions $f_i^a$ on $B_{8Sr_i}(a)$ such that, with $q^a=d_a^2/2$, $$\Delta f_i^a=a_i:=n+C_0\kappa r_i^2,
 \qquad f_i^a-q^a\in W^{1,2}_0(B_{8Sr_i}(a)).
 \label{pi:poisson-definition}$$ Here $C_0$ depends only on $n,S$ and is the same for every center and every scale. The following bounds hold: $$\begin{aligned}
 0\le q^a-f_i^a&\le Cr_i^2,
 &\sup_{B_{4Sr_i}(a)}|\nabla f_i^a|&\le Cr_i,
 \label{pi:poisson-boundedness}\\
 \int_{B_{8Sr_i}(a)}|\nabla(f_i^a-q^a)|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 &\le Cr_i^{n+2}P_i(a),
 &\int_{B_{8Sr_i}(a)}|\nabla(f_i^a-q^a)|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 &\le Cr_i^{n+1}P_i(a),
 \label{pi:poisson-errors}\\
 \mathcal B_\kappa(f_i^a,1)(B_{2Sr_i}(a))
 &\le Cr_i^nP_i(a).
 \label{pi:poisson-shifted-mass}
\end{aligned}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-poisson-pinching"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-poisson-pinching</summary>

*Proof.* Choose $C_0$ sufficiently large that Laplacian comparison gives $$\Delta q^a\le(n+C_0\kappa r_i^2)\mathfrak m
 \quad\hbox{on }B_{16Sr_i}(a).$$ This is possible because $d_a\le16Sr_i$ there. Take the sequence late enough that $C_0\kappa\le1$. Define the positive measure $$\mu_i^a=a_i\mathfrak m-\Delta q^a
 \quad\hbox{on }B_{16Sr_i}(a),
 \qquad
 P_i(a)=r_i^{-n}\mu_i^a(B_{8Sr_i}(a))+\kappa r_i^2.$$ Here the measure comparison for the squared distance is on the whole ball, including its center; see (Gigli 2015, Corollary 5.15, equation (5.29)) <a id="pm-cite-44"></a>[[Gigli15](#ref-Gigli15)]. The source restricted to $B_{8Sr_i}(a)$ is a continuous functional on $W^{1,2}_0$ since it is the difference of a bounded density and the distributional Laplacian of the Sobolev function $q^a$. The Dirichlet variational problem therefore gives $h_i^a\in W^{1,2}_0$ with $-\Delta h_i^a=\mu_i^a$. Testing its negative part gives $h_i^a\ge0$, and $f_i^a=q^a-h_i^a$ satisfies <a href="#pi:poisson-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:poisson-definition">[pi:poisson-definition]</a>.

Here is the mass estimate, including its normalization. Set $V_a(t)=\mathfrak m(B_t(a))$ and $A_a(t)=t^{-n}V_a(t)$. Radial testing of the measure-valued Laplacian of $q^a$, with $|\nabla d_a|=1$ almost everywhere off $a$, gives $$\Delta q^a(B_t(a))=tV_a'(t)
 \quad\hbox{for almost every }t.$$ Equivalently, this follows by approximating radial indicators in $-\int t\psi'(t)V_a'(t)\,\mathrm dt$; the boundary term at zero vanishes by the local volume upper bound. Thus $$\mu_i^a(B_t(a))=(a_i-n)V_a(t)-t^{n+1}A_a'(t)$$ at those radii. Positivity and integration for $8Sr_i<t<16Sr_i$ imply <a id="pi:volume-drop"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 r_i^{-n}\mu_i^a(B_{8Sr_i}(a))
 &\le C\int_{8Sr_i}^{16Sr_i}
       \frac{\mu_i^a(B_t(a))}{t^{n+1}}\,\mathrm dt\notag\\
 &\le C\{A_a(8Sr_i)-A_a(16Sr_i)+\kappa r_i^2\}.
 \label{pi:volume-drop}
\end{aligned}$$ The last step uses $V_a(t)\le Ct^n$ on these fixed rescaled balls. For a finite dyadic sum, the volume terms on the right telescope exactly to $A_a(8Sr_N)-A_a(16S)$. The noncollapsed Bishop inequality gives $\lim_{t\downarrow0}A_a(t)\le\omega_n$ at every center, where $\omega_n$ is the Euclidean unit-ball volume. The fixed-radius volume convergence in the preceding section gives $A_a(16S)\ge\omega_n-o(1)$ uniformly in $a\in B_4(p)$. Since $\sum_i\kappa r_i^2\le4\kappa/3$, this proves <a href="#pi:poisson-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:poisson-sum">[pi:poisson-sum]</a>. In particular this argument does not assume that the neighboring centers are regular.

For completeness, the bound for the Dirichlet solution can be obtained with uniform constants directly from <a href="#pi:dirichlet-sobolev" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:dirichlet-sobolev">[pi:dirichlet-sobolev]</a>. Work in units $r_i=1$. Let $\tau$ solve $-\Delta\tau=1$ with zero boundary values on $B_{8S}(a)$. If $Y(k)=\mathfrak m(\tau>k)$, testing with $(\tau-k)_+$ and applying Sobolev and Hölder gives, for $l>k$, $$Y(l)\le C(l-k)^{-2d}Y(k)^{2d-1}.$$ Put $k_j=K(1-2^{-j})$. Since $2d-1>1$, choose $K$ using only $C,d,Y(0)$ so that this recursion implies $Y(k_j)\le Y(0)2^{-jd/(d-1)}$ by induction. Then $\tau\le K$. The harmonic function $f_i^a+a_i\tau$ has the nonnegative boundary values $q^a$, so the weak maximum principle gives $f_i^a\ge-a_i\tau$. Together with $h_i^a\ge0$ this gives $0\le h_i^a\le q^a+a_i\tau\le C$. Rescaling proves the first bound in <a href="#pi:poisson-boundedness" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:poisson-boundedness">[pi:poisson-boundedness]</a>.

The local Poisson gradient estimate gives $|\nabla f_i^a|\le C$ throughout $B_{4S}(a)$ in these units, since the values and the constant right side are bounded. More explicitly, for each $q\in B_{4S}(a)$ apply the estimate on $B_{S/4}(q)$; its eightfold enlargement $B_{2S}(q)$ is contained in $B_{6S}(a)$, strictly inside the Dirichlet domain $B_{8S}(a)$. The estimate and its uniform local dependence follow from (Kell 2013; Jiang 2014) <a id="pm-cite-45"></a>[[Kell13](#ref-Kell13), [Jiang14](#ref-Jiang14)]; the local test-function interpretation is supplied by Lemma <a href="#co:local-calculus" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:local-calculus">3</a> (see also (Connell et al. 2021) <a id="pm-cite-46"></a>[[CDNZPSW](#ref-CDNZPSW)]). Apply Lemma <a href="#pi:positive-source" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:positive-source">15</a> to $h_i^a$ on this same rescaled Dirichlet domain. Its source mass is at most $P_i(a)$. This proves both estimates in <a href="#pi:poisson-errors" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:poisson-errors">[pi:poisson-errors]</a>, including their displayed powers of $r_i$.

It remains to bound the full shifted measure. Choose a nonnegative test cutoff $\chi$, equal to one on $B_{2Sr_i}(a)$ and supported in $B_{4Sr_i}(a)$, with $|\Delta\chi|\le Cr_i^{-2}$. Since $h=q^a-f_i^a$ and $|\nabla q^a|\le8Sr_i$, we have $$\int\left|\tfrac12|\nabla f_i^a|^2-q^a\right|\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 \le\int\bigl(|\nabla q^a||\nabla h|
                  +\tfrac12|\nabla h|^2\bigr)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 \le Cr_i^{n+2}P_i(a).$$ The integral can be restricted to the cutoff support or taken over the Dirichlet domain. Testing the definition of $M$ and using $\Delta q^a=a_i\mathfrak m-\mu_i^a$ now gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \int\chi\,\mathrm d\mathcal B_\kappa(f_i^a,1)
 &=\frac12\int|\nabla f_i^a|^2\Delta\chi\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
   +\kappa\int\chi|\nabla f_i^a|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
   -(2a_i-n)\int\chi\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\\
 &=(n-a_i)\int\chi\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m-\int\chi\,\mathrm d\mu_i^a
   +\kappa\int\chi|\nabla f_i^a|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
   +O(r_i^nP_i(a))\\
 &\le Cr_i^nP_i(a).
\end{aligned}$$ The first two terms in the penultimate line are nonpositive, and the curvature term is bounded by $C\kappa r_i^{n+2}$, already included in $Cr_i^nP_i(a)$. Positivity of $\mathcal B_\kappa(f_i^a,1)$ proves <a href="#pi:poisson-shifted-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:poisson-shifted-mass">[pi:poisson-shifted-mass]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-poisson-units"></a>
<div id="pi:poisson-units" class="remark">

*Remark 17* (The units of the Poisson estimates). Under $d'=r_i^{-1}d$, $\mathfrak m'=r_i^{-n}\mathfrak m$ and $\widehat f_i^a=r_i^{-2}f_i^a$, one has $\Delta'\widehat f_i^a=a_i$ and $\kappa'=\kappa r_i^2$. The measure $\mathcal B_{\kappa'}(\widehat f_i^a,1)$ is $r_i^{-n}\mathcal B_\kappa(f_i^a,1)$. Consequently all the Poisson bounds above have size $CP_i(a)$ in their natural rescaled units. In particular, by <a href="#pi:shifted-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:shifted-triangle">[pi:shifted-triangle]</a>, differences of two replacements at the same scale are harmonic and have Bochner mass at most $C(P_i(x)+P_i(z))$ on any of the common interior balls. This estimate controls the full measure, including its possible singular part.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### Radial differentiation of the coordinate span

The Poisson estimates provide a summable scalar budget. We next fit the radial derivatives by affine combinations of the harmonic coordinates and distribute the errors over neighboring scales.

We first record the weak cancellation used to replace a radial derivative by a harmonic function.

<a id="stmt-pi-weak-cancellation"></a>
<div id="pi:weak-cancellation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 18** (Weak radial cancellation). *Suppose that $f$ has constant Laplacian and $a$ is harmonic on a ball on which both functions have bounded gradients. Put $g_a=\langle\nabla f,\nabla a\rangle$. For interior Sobolev tests $\phi\in W^{1,2}_0$ one has <a id="pi:weak-cancellation-equation"></a>$$\int\langle\nabla g_a,\nabla\phi\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 =2\int(\operatorname{Hess}f-g)(\nabla a,\nabla\phi)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m.
 \label{pi:weak-cancellation-equation}$$ The identity is local, with the right side interpreted on a slightly larger interior ball if a cutoff is needed.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-weak-cancellation"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-weak-cancellation</summary>

*Proof.* Local test regularity and the Hessian product rule give $$\nabla g_a=(\operatorname{Hess}f)(\nabla a,\cdot)
             +(\operatorname{Hess}a)(\nabla f,\cdot).$$ For a test function $\phi$, express each of the two integrated Hessian terms by differentiating $\langle\nabla a,\nabla\phi\rangle$ or $\langle\nabla f,\nabla\phi\rangle$ and integrating by parts. The terms with $\operatorname{Hess}\phi$ cancel, leaving $$\begin{aligned}
 &\int\operatorname{Hess}a(\nabla f,\nabla\phi)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
  -\int\operatorname{Hess}f(\nabla a,\nabla\phi)\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\\
 &\hspace{1cm}
 =-\int\Delta f\,\langle\nabla a,\nabla\phi\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
   +\int\Delta a\,\langle\nabla f,\nabla\phi\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 =0.
\end{aligned}$$ The constancy of $\Delta f$ and harmonicity of $a$ are both used in the last equality. This proves the identity with $\operatorname{Hess}f$ on the right. Subtracting the metric tensor changes its right side by $2\int\langle\nabla a,\nabla\phi\rangle=0$. Both sides are continuous in the $W^{1,2}_0$ norm, by the bounded gradient factors and the local $L^2$ Hessian bounds. Density therefore extends the identity to the stated tests. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-affine-improvement"></a>
<div id="pi:affine-improvement" class="lemma">

**Lemma 19** (Harmonic affine improvement). *There is an integer $m\ge3$, depending only on $n,S$, such that, with $\theta=2^{-m}$, the following holds for all sufficiently late spaces, all $i$ and all $a\in B_4(p)$. If $w$ is harmonic on $B_{Sr_i}(a)$, then there is an affine combination $F$ of the components of $u$ with <a id="pi:affine-improvement-equation"></a>$$\|w-F\|_{2,B_{S\theta r_i}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le\tfrac12\theta^{3/2}
       \|w\|_{2,B_{Sr_i}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}.
 \label{pi:affine-improvement-equation}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-affine-improvement"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-affine-improvement</summary>

*Proof.* In a Euclidean ball, interior derivative estimates and Taylor’s formula at the center give $$\|w-w(0)-\nabla w(0)\cdot X\|_{2,B_{S\theta}}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le C_n\theta^2\|w\|_{2,B_S}^{\mathrm{av}},
 \qquad \theta<1/4.$$ Choose $m$ so that $C_n\theta^2<\theta^{3/2}/4$. If the asserted conclusion failed along increasingly late spaces, rescale by $r_i$, normalize the nonzero norm of $w$ to one, and apply interior harmonic compactness (Ambrosio and Honda 2018) <a id="pm-cite-47"></a>[[AH18](#ref-AH18)]. The limit is Euclidean harmonic on $B_S$, with norm at most one there. The normalized coordinate columns converge locally uniformly to orthonormal affine Euclidean coordinates, by Lemma <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a>. An affine combination of these columns therefore approximates the affine Taylor polynomial of the limit uniformly on $B_{S\theta}$. Strong convergence on this smaller ball contradicts the strict Euclidean estimate. This contradiction gives a single late-space threshold for every center, scale, and harmonic $w$. If the normalizing norm is zero, the assertion is immediate. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-radial-invariance"></a>
<div id="pi:radial-invariance" class="lemma">

<a id="pi:pinching-summability"></a>
<a id="pi:radial-invariance-equation"></a>
**Lemma 20** (Summable radial invariance). *There are functions $E_i(a)>0$, for $a\in B_4(p)$, such that $$E_i(a)\ge P_i(a),\qquad
 \sup_a\sum_{i=0}^\infty E_i(a)=o(1),\qquad
 \inf_a E_i(a)>0\quad\hbox{for each fixed }i.
 \label{pi:pinching-summability}$$ Let $T=R_i(y)^{-1/2}$ with $d(a,y)\le C_{\mathrm{sep}}r_i$, and put $U=T(u-u(a))/r_i$. In the metric $r_i^{-1}d$, with $\widehat f_i^a=f_i^a/r_i^2$, there is a matrix $A_i^a(T)$ satisfying $\|A_i^a(T)-\mathrm{Id}\|=o(1)$ and $$\left\|\langle\nabla\widehat f_i^a,\nabla U\rangle
       -A_i^a(T)(U-U(a))\right\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{S/2}(a))}
 \le C\sqrt{E_i(a)}.
 \label{pi:radial-invariance-equation}$$ The ball and all derivatives in this display use the rescaled metric. Arbitrary additive constants may be used in $U$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-radial-invariance"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-radial-invariance</summary>

<a id="pi:raw-radial-error"></a>
<a id="pi:radial-harmonic-correction"></a>
*Proof.* *Harmonic correction and affine fits.* We first work in the unrescaled units of the current member of the sequence and fix a center $a$ and a covector $b$. Define $$g_b=\langle\nabla q^a,\nabla(b\cdot u)\rangle,
 \qquad
 g_{j,b}=\langle\nabla f_j^a,\nabla(b\cdot u)\rangle.$$ The Poisson error and <a href="#pi:coordinate-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:coordinate-input">[pi:coordinate-input]</a> imply $$\|g_b-g_{j,b}\|_{2,B_{Sr_j}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le Cr_j\sigma_j^a(b)\sqrt{P_j(a)}.
 \label{pi:raw-radial-error}$$ By Lemma <a href="#pi:weak-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:weak-cancellation">18</a>, the equation for $g_{j,b}$ on this ball has divergence source $2(\operatorname{Hess}f_j^a-g)(\nabla(b\cdot u),\cdot)$. Its $L^2$ norm is at most $Cr_j^{n/2}\sigma_j^a(b)\sqrt{P_j(a)}$. Solve this divergence equation with zero boundary values to obtain $\xi_{j,b}\in W^{1,2}_0(B_{Sr_j}(a))$. Testing with $\xi_{j,b}$ and then applying Poincaré gives $$\|\xi_{j,b}\|_{2,B_{Sr_j}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 +r_j\|\nabla\xi_{j,b}\|_{2,B_{Sr_j}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le Cr_j\sigma_j^a(b)\sqrt{P_j(a)}.
 \label{pi:radial-harmonic-correction}$$ In particular $g_{j,b}-\xi_{j,b}$ is harmonic on that ball.

<a id="pi:fit-recurrence"></a>
Let $F_{j,b}$ be the $L^2(B_{Sr_j}(a))$ orthogonal projection of $g_b$ onto the finite-dimensional space of affine combinations of $u$, and set $$e_j(b)=r_j^{-1}
       \|g_b-F_{j,b}\|_{2,B_{Sr_j}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}.$$ The affine space is nondegenerate by harmonic normalization, so these projections exist uniquely as functions and depend linearly on $b$. Apply Lemma <a href="#pi:affine-improvement" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:affine-improvement">19</a> to $g_{j,b}-\xi_{j,b}-F_{j,b}$. The two errors in <a href="#pi:raw-radial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:raw-radial-error">[pi:raw-radial-error]</a> and <a href="#pi:radial-harmonic-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:radial-harmonic-correction">[pi:radial-harmonic-correction]</a>, restricted to the smaller ball, acquire only the fixed volume factor depending on $m,S$. Dividing by $r_{j+m}=\theta r_j$ gives $$e_{j+m}(b)\le\theta^{1/2}e_j(b)
                  +C\sigma_j^a(b)\sqrt{P_j(a)}.
 \label{pi:fit-recurrence}$$ The factor $1/2$ in the harmonic improvement leaves more than the needed margin in the coefficient of $e_j(b)$.

<a id="pi:qualitative-radial-error"></a>
*Qualitative convergence.* We will also use the uniform estimate $$e_j(b)\le\varepsilon\sigma_j^a(b),\qquad\varepsilon=o(1).
 \label{pi:qualitative-radial-error}$$ Here are details concerning the distance gradients in this assertion. In units $r_j=1$, put $q=q^a/r_j^2$, normalize the coordinate column at $a$, and write $V=R_j(a)^{-1/2}(u-u(a))/r_j$. On every fixed interior ball, $V$ converges to orthonormal Euclidean coordinates and $q^a/r_j^2-|V|^2/2\to0$ uniformly. The norms of the gradients of these two functions have the same limiting squared integrals: for the distance function use $|\nabla(d_a^2/2)|=d_a$, and for $|V|^2/2$ use the strong Gram convergence. Their cross term has the same limit. Indeed, for a compactly supported Lipschitz cutoff $\chi$, $$\int\chi\langle\nabla(q-|V|^2/2),\nabla(|V|^2/2)\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 =-\int(q-|V|^2/2)
       \operatorname{div}(\chi\nabla(|V|^2/2))\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m=o(1),$$ because $\Delta(|V|^2/2)=\sum_k|\nabla V_k|^2$ and the relevant gradients are uniformly bounded. Thus the two gradients are close in local $L^2$. Taking scalar products with $\nabla V$ and using strong Gram convergence shows $$\langle\nabla q,\nabla V\rangle-V\longrightarrow0
 \quad\hbox{in local }L^2.$$ This supplies an affine candidate in the defining infimum for $e_j(b)$. A contradiction subsequence over the center, scale, and unit normalized covector makes the estimate uniform, proving <a href="#pi:qualitative-radial-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:qualitative-radial-error">[pi:qualitative-radial-error]</a>.

<a id="pi:smoothed-fit-error"></a>
*Summable squared errors.* Choose a positive number $\eta=o(1)$ with $\eta\ge\varepsilon^2$; for example, add the reciprocal of the sequence index to $\varepsilon^2$. Fix $\gamma=1/8$. Iterating <a href="#pi:fit-recurrence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:fit-recurrence">[pi:fit-recurrence]</a> in each of the finitely many residue classes modulo $m$, and using <a href="#pi:coordinate-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:coordinate-input">[pi:coordinate-input]</a>, yields $$e_i(b)\le C\sigma_i^a(b)\sqrt{\widetilde P_i(a)},\qquad
 \widetilde P_i(a)=\eta2^{-\gamma i}
          +\sum_{j=0}^i2^{-\gamma(i-j)}P_j(a).
 \label{pi:smoothed-fit-error}$$ To see the exponent explicitly, the coefficient of a source at $j<i$ before squaring is bounded by $C_m2^{-(1/2-a_0)(i-j)}$. The initial error has the same decay times $\varepsilon$. Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz bounds the square of the source sum by an exponential convolution of $P_j$. Since $a_0=1/16$ and $\gamma=1/8<1/2-a_0$, weakening that convolution gives exactly <a href="#pi:smoothed-fit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:smoothed-fit-error">[pi:smoothed-fit-error]</a>. This calculation also covers the finitely many initial indices.

<a id="pi:inward-constant-sum"></a>
<a id="pi:definition-E"></a>
*Removing the constant term.* The constants in the affine fits must be removed with a quantitative bound. Put $c_j=F_{j,b}(a)$. On $B_{Sr_{j+1}}(a)$, the difference of consecutive fits has averaged $L^2$ norm at most $Cr_j(e_j(b)+e_{j+1}(b))$. Uniform affine nondegeneracy, or harmonic interior evaluation on this ball, therefore gives $$|c_j-c_{j+1}|\le Cr_j(e_j(b)+e_{j+1}(b)).$$ Moreover $|c_j|\le Cr_j\sigma_j^a(b)$. Indeed the raw radial function has this averaged size, its projection has no larger norm, and affine evaluation at the center is uniformly bounded. The growth exponent $a_0<1$ implies $c_j\to0$ as $j\to\infty$. Consequently, using <a href="#pi:smoothed-fit-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:smoothed-fit-error">[pi:smoothed-fit-error]</a>, $$\begin{aligned}
 \frac{|c_i|}{r_i\sigma_i^a(b)}
 &\le C\sum_{l\ge i}2^{-(1-a_0)(l-i)}
       \bigl(\sqrt{\widetilde P_l(a)}
                +\sqrt{\widetilde P_{l+1}(a)}\bigr).
 \label{pi:inward-constant-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ Fix $\beta=1/16$ and define, with a sufficiently large fixed constant $C_E$, $$E_i(a)=C_E\sum_{l=0}^\infty
                  2^{-\beta|i-l|}\widetilde P_l(a).
 \label{pi:definition-E}$$ Weighted Cauchy–Schwarz in <a href="#pi:inward-constant-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:inward-constant-sum">[pi:inward-constant-sum]</a> then gives $|c_i|\le Cr_i\sigma_i^a(b)\sqrt{E_i(a)}$. The two geometric convolution kernels have finite sums, so $$\sup_a\sum_iE_i(a)
 \le C\left(\eta+\sup_a\sum_iP_i(a)\right)=o(1).$$ Also $E_i(a)\ge P_i(a)$ after increasing $C_E$, and the term $l=0$ gives the center-independent positive floor $E_i(a)\ge C_E\eta2^{-\beta i}$.

*Gradient control and normalization.* Combining the value estimates already proved gives $$\|g_{i,b}-(F_{i,b}-c_i)\|_{2,B_{Sr_i}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le Cr_i\sigma_i^a(b)\sqrt{E_i(a)}.$$ The difference $g_{i,b}-F_{i,b}$ satisfies the divergence equation of Lemma <a href="#pi:weak-cancellation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:weak-cancellation">18</a>, because $F_{i,b}$ is harmonic. Test it against its product with a squared Lipschitz cutoff, equal to one on $B_{Sr_i/2}(a)$. Cauchy–Schwarz and absorption give the interior energy estimate $$\|\nabla(g_{i,b}-F_{i,b})\|_{2,B_{Sr_i/2}(a)}^{\mathrm{av}}
 \le C\sigma_i^a(b)\sqrt{E_i(a)}.$$ Here the value term is divided by $r_i$, and the divergence source has the bound used in <a href="#pi:radial-harmonic-correction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:radial-harmonic-correction">[pi:radial-harmonic-correction]</a>.

For $T=R_i(a)^{-1/2}$, apply these scalar estimates to its rows, rescale, and stack the resulting affine fits. Their constants at $a$ have been removed, so they give a matrix $A_i^a(T)$ and <a href="#pi:radial-invariance-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:radial-invariance-equation">[pi:radial-invariance-equation]</a>. The qualitative radial convergence proved above, the Poisson error, and affine nondegeneracy imply $\|A_i^a(T)-\mathrm{Id}\|\le C(\varepsilon+\sqrt{P_i(a)}+
\sqrt{E_i(a)})=o(1)$ uniformly. Finally, replacing $R_i(a)^{-1/2}$ by $R_i(y)^{-1/2}$ conjugates this matrix by $R_i(y)^{-1/2}R_i(a)^{1/2}$. This matrix and its inverse have uniformly bounded norms by fixed-distance normalization comparability. Thus both the quantitative bound and $A_i^a(T)-\mathrm{Id}=o(1)$ persist. Translating $U$ changes neither side when its center value is subtracted. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### Two centers and the sharp coordinate projection

In Euclidean space, the difference $q^z-q^x$ of the squared-distance potentials is affine, with gradient $x-z$. We need a quantitative version of both facts for the equal-Laplacian Poisson replacements: their harmonic difference must be close to an affine coordinate function, and its coefficient must be close to the coordinate difference of the centers.

Fix a configuration <a href="#pi:center-configuration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:center-configuration">[pi:center-configuration]</a>. In this subsection take $r_i$ as the length unit and replace the measure by $r_i^{-n}\mathfrak m$. Write $$f^x=f_i^x/r_i^2,\qquad f^z=f_i^z/r_i^2,
 \qquad U=R_i(y)^{-1/2}(u-u(z))/r_i,
 \qquad s^2=E_i(x)+E_i(z).$$ All balls and derivatives until the final scale conversion are in these units. We write $M$ for the Bochner measure with lower-bound parameter $\kappa r_i^2$ in these units, and $g$ for the rescaled metric tensor. The equal-source construction gives $\Delta f^x=\Delta f^z=a_i$. Put <a id="pi:pair-notation"></a>$$v=f^z-f^x,\qquad
 L_a w=\langle\nabla f^a,\nabla w\rangle\ (a=x,z),\qquad
 h=\langle\nabla v,\nabla U\rangle.
 \label{pi:pair-notation}$$ The domain nesting in <a href="#pi:radii" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:radii">[pi:radii]</a> gives $$B_{8R}(z)\subset B_{S/2}(x)\cap B_{S/2}(z),
 \qquad
 B_{2L+4}(y)\subset B_{R/2}(z).$$ All these inclusions are strict with a fixed margin, since $d(x,z)\le2C_{\mathrm{sep}}$ and $S/2=16R$.

The commutator below first identifies $h$ with $U(x)-U(z)$ up to $O_{L^2}(s)$. The sharp projection then gives an affine function $c_0+b\cdot U$ whose gradient differs from that of $v$ by $O_{L^2}(s)$ on the required interior ball. Averaging against the normalized Gram matrix recovers $b=U(x)-U(z)+O(s)$. These two conclusions transfer the $Cs^2$ Bochner bound for $v$ to the coordinate function in the direction $U(x)-U(z)$.

<a id="stmt-pi-commutator"></a>
<div id="pi:commutator" class="lemma">

<a id="pi:pair-poisson-bounds"></a>
<a id="pi:constant-direction"></a>
**Lemma 21** (The two-center commutator). *On $B_{8R}(z)$ one has $$\Delta v=0,\qquad
 |\nabla v|\le C,\qquad
 M(v)(B_{8R}(z))\le Cs^2,
 \label{pi:pair-poisson-bounds}$$ and $$\|h-(U(x)-U(z))\|_{L^2(B_{8R}(z))}\le Cs.
 \label{pi:constant-direction}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-commutator"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-commutator</summary>

<a id="pi:commutator-affine"></a>
*Proof.* The first assertions follow from the common value of the Laplacian, the Poisson gradient bounds, and <a href="#pi:shifted-triangle" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:shifted-triangle">[pi:shifted-triangle]</a>; see Remark <a href="#pi:poisson-units" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:poisson-units">17</a>. In particular $$\|\operatorname{Hess}v\|_{L^2(B_{8R}(z))}
 +\|\operatorname{Hess}f^z-g\|_{L^2(B_{8R}(z))}\le Cs.$$ Let $A_x,A_z$ be the matrices in Lemma <a href="#pi:radial-invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:radial-invariance">20</a> for the common normalization $R_i(y)^{-1/2}$. Put $$\delta=U(x)-U(z),\qquad B=A_z-A_x,\qquad c=A_x\delta.$$ Since $U(z)=0$, subtraction of the two radial-invariance identities gives $$h=BU+c+e,\qquad
 \|e\|_{W^{1,2}(B_{8R}(z))}\le Cs.
 \label{pi:commutator-affine}$$ All coordinate gradients are bounded on this ball, and the values of $U$, $\delta$, and $c$ are bounded by constants depending on the fixed radii. Also $A_x,A_z=\mathrm{Id}+o(1)$.

<a id="pi:commutator-equation"></a>
Set $L_vw=\langle\nabla v,\nabla w\rangle$. Differentiate the radial identity $L_zU=A_zU+O_{W^{1,2}}(s)$ along $\nabla v$. Its error remains $O_{L^2}(s)$ because $|\nabla v|$ is bounded, so $$L_vL_zU=A_zh+O_{L^2}(s).$$ The Hessian product rule separately gives the exact identity $$L_vL_zU-L_zh
 =\operatorname{Hess}f^z(\nabla v,\nabla U)
       -\operatorname{Hess}v(\nabla f^z,\nabla U).$$ The last displayed Hessian bounds and the bounded gradient factors turn its right side into $h+O_{L^2}(s)$. Thus $$L_zh=(A_z-\mathrm{Id})h+O_{L^2}(s).
 \label{pi:commutator-equation}$$ Every derivative here is legitimate in $L^2$ by local test regularity and the gradient-product rule; no classical differentiability of the metric is being used.

<a id="pi:delta-from-c"></a>
Insert <a href="#pi:commutator-affine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:commutator-affine">[pi:commutator-affine]</a> on both sides of <a href="#pi:commutator-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:commutator-equation">[pi:commutator-equation]</a>, and use the radial identity once more to differentiate $BU$. This gives $$\bigl(BA_z-(A_z-\mathrm{Id})B\bigr)U-(A_z-\mathrm{Id})c
 =O_{L^2}(s).$$ Affine nondegeneracy, for example on $B_R(z)$, bounds its linear and constant coefficients separately: <a id="pi:commutator-coefficients"></a>$$\|BA_z-(A_z-\mathrm{Id})B\|+|(A_z-\mathrm{Id})c|\le Cs.
 \label{pi:commutator-coefficients}$$ Writing $C_z=A_z-\mathrm{Id}$, the first operator is $B+BC_z-C_zB$. Consequently $$(1-2\|C_z\|)\|B\|
 \le\|B+BC_z-C_zB\|\le Cs.$$ For sufficiently late spaces, $\|C_z\|<1/4$, and hence $\|B\|\le Cs$. Next $$(A_x-\mathrm{Id})c=(A_z-\mathrm{Id})c-Bc=O(s).$$ Since $c=A_x\delta$ and $A_x^{-1}$ is bounded, this yields $$\delta-c=-A_x^{-1}(A_x-\mathrm{Id})c=O(s).
 \label{pi:delta-from-c}$$ Finally <a href="#pi:commutator-affine" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:commutator-affine">[pi:commutator-affine]</a>, the bounded coordinate values, and <a href="#pi:delta-from-c" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:delta-from-c">[pi:delta-from-c]</a> prove <a href="#pi:constant-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:constant-direction">[pi:constant-direction]</a> on the full ball $B_{8R}(z)$. ◻

</details>

The next lemma supplies the norm bound on a ball larger than the projection domain. This is the step which permits normalization of a potentially very small projected residual.

<a id="stmt-pi-radial-transport"></a>
<div id="pi:radial-transport" class="lemma">

<a id="pi:transport-conclusion"></a>
**Lemma 22** (Radial transport of an unnormalized residual). *Work in the rescaled units above. Suppose that $w$ is a bounded Sobolev function on $B_{4R}(z)$ with $\|w\|_\infty\le C_1$, and that <a id="pi:transport-hypothesis"></a>$$L_zw=w+\ell\cdot U+k+e_w,
 \qquad \|e_w\|_{L^2(B_{4R}(z))}\le C_1s.
 \label{pi:transport-hypothesis}$$ Put $\alpha=\|w\|_{L^2(B_R(z))}$. If $|\ell|+|k|\le C_1(\alpha+s)$, then $$\|w\|_{L^2(B_{2R}(z))}\le C(\alpha+s),
 \label{pi:transport-conclusion}$$ where $C$ is independent of $\alpha,s$. The constants may depend on $C_1$ and the already fixed radii and coordinate bounds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-radial-transport"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-radial-transport</summary>

<a id="pi:transport-divergence"></a>
*Proof.* The Sobolev product rule and $\Delta f^z=a_i$ give $$\operatorname{div}(w^2\nabla f^z)
 =(a_i+2)w^2+wF,\qquad
 F=2(\ell\cdot U+k+e_w),
 \label{pi:transport-divergence}$$ with $\|F\|_{L^2(B_{3R}(z))}\le C(\alpha+s)$. The identity is a weak identity: boundedness of $w$, its Sobolev regularity, and the bounded gradient of $f^z$ justify the product and its testing with compactly supported Lipschitz functions.

<a id="pi:transport-cutoff-sign"></a>
Here is a particular cutoff with the required sign. For $t\ge0$ let $k_R(t)$ equal one for $t\le2R$, decrease linearly to zero on $[2R,3R]$, and equal zero for $t\ge3R$. Choose $A_0=2(n+4)/(R-1)$ and put $$\phi(t)=
 \begin{cases}
 1,&0\le t\le R,\\
 e^{-A_0(t-R)}k_R(t),&R<t<3R,\\
 0,&t\ge3R.
 \end{cases}$$ This is a bounded nonnegative Lipschitz function. It is bounded below by $e^{-A_0R}>0$ on $[0,2R]$, and on $R<t<3R$ it satisfies $-\phi'(t)\ge A_0\phi(t)$. Since $n\le a_i\le n+1$ and $R>1$, the choice of $A_0$ implies $$-t\phi'(t)-(a_i+2)\phi(t)
 \ge |\phi'(t)|+\phi(t)
 \quad\hbox{for almost every }R<t<3R.
 \label{pi:transport-cutoff-sign}$$ The derivative and the cutoff are bounded by fixed constants.

<a id="pi:transport-energy"></a>
<a id="pi:transport-square-error"></a>
Write $t=d_z$ and $$e_f=\langle\nabla d_z,\nabla f^z\rangle-d_z,
 \qquad
 \mathcal A=\int_{R<d_z<3R}(|\phi'|+\phi)w^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m.$$ The Poisson gradient error gives $\|e_f\|_{L^2(B_{3R}(z))}\le Cs$, because $\nabla(d_z^2/2)=d_z\nabla d_z$. Test <a href="#pi:transport-divergence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:transport-divergence">[pi:transport-divergence]</a> against $\phi(d_z)$. Using <a href="#pi:transport-cutoff-sign" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:transport-cutoff-sign">[pi:transport-cutoff-sign]</a>, and moving the inner-ball term to the right, gives $$\mathcal A\le C\alpha^2
   +\left|\int\phi wF\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right|
   +\left|\int_{R<d_z<3R}\phi'e_f w^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right|.
 \label{pi:transport-energy}$$ The decisive coefficient error has the bound $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\int_{R<d_z<3R}\phi'e_f w^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right|
 &\le\|w\|_\infty
       \left(\int|\phi'|w^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right)^{1/2}
       \left(\int|\phi'|e_f^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right)^{1/2}\notag\\
 &\le Cs\sqrt{\mathcal A}
 \le\tfrac14\mathcal A+Cs^2.
 \label{pi:transport-square-error}
\end{aligned}$$ All integrals in the middle line are over the annulus. For the $F$ term, the inner ball contributes at most $C\alpha(\alpha+s)$, while the annulus contributes at most $C\sqrt{\mathcal A}(\alpha+s)$. Another application of Young’s inequality therefore gives $$\left|\int\phi wF\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\right|
 \le\tfrac14\mathcal A+C(\alpha^2+s^2).$$ Substitution in <a href="#pi:transport-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:transport-energy">[pi:transport-energy]</a> proves $\mathcal A\le C(\alpha^2+s^2)$. The positive lower bound for $\phi$ up to radius $2R$ now gives <a href="#pi:transport-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:transport-conclusion">[pi:transport-conclusion]</a>.

In particular, the fixed bound for $\|w\|_\infty$ in <a href="#pi:transport-square-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:transport-square-error">[pi:transport-square-error]</a> is used before $w$ is divided by $\alpha$. The conclusion is quadratic in $s$ at the level of squared norms. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-sharp-projection"></a>
<div id="pi:sharp-projection" class="lemma">

<a id="pi:sharp-projection-equation"></a>
**Lemma 23** (Sharp approximation by the harmonic coordinate span). *For all sufficiently late spaces, there is a vector $b\in\mathbb R^n$ with $|b|\le C$ such that $$\|\nabla(v-b\cdot U)\|_{L^2(B_{2L+4}(y))}\le Cs.
 \label{pi:sharp-projection-equation}$$ The constants and the late-space threshold are uniform in the configuration <a href="#pi:center-configuration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:center-configuration">[pi:center-configuration]</a> and the dyadic scale.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-sharp-projection"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-sharp-projection</summary>

*Proof.* Subtract a constant from $v$ so that $v(z)=0$; this does not change any gradient, Laplacian, or Bochner measure. Its bounded gradient gives a uniform bound for its values on $B_{8R}(z)$. Let $c_0+b\cdot U$ be its $L^2(B_R(z))$ orthogonal projection onto the affine coordinate span, and put $$w=v-c_0-b\cdot U,\qquad
 \alpha=\|w\|_{L^2(B_R(z))}.$$ Affine nondegeneracy and the bounds for $v,U$ give $|c_0|+|b|\le C$. The function $w$ is harmonic on $B_{8R}(z)$ and has uniformly bounded values and gradients there.

<a id="pi:projected-euler-equation"></a>
<a id="pi:forcing-coefficient-bound"></a>
<a id="pi:projection-outer-bound"></a>
The gradient product rule and the Poisson estimates yield <a id="pi:euler-gradient"></a>$$\nabla(L_zv-v)
 =(\operatorname{Hess}f^z-g)(\nabla v,\cdot)
    +\operatorname{Hess}v(\nabla f^z,\cdot),
 \qquad
 \|\nabla(L_zv-v)\|_{L^2(B_{8R}(z))}\le Cs.
 \label{pi:euler-gradient}$$ Poincaré on $B_{4R}(z)$, using the gradient on $B_{8R}(z)$, therefore gives a constant $k_v$ such that $L_zv-v=k_v+O_{L^2(B_{4R}(z))}(s)$. Subtracting the projection, and using $L_zU=A_zU+O_{W^{1,2}}(s)$, gives $$L_zw=w+\ell\cdot U+k+e_w,
 \qquad
 \|e_w\|_{L^2(B_{4R}(z))}\le Cs,
 \label{pi:projected-euler-equation}$$ where $\ell^T=b^T(\mathrm{Id}-A_z)$ and $k=k_v+c_0$. We need a bound for these coefficients in terms of the small residual norm. Caccioppoli for the harmonic function $w$ gives $$\|\nabla w\|_{L^2(B_{R/2}(z))}\le C\alpha.$$ The bounded gradient of $f^z$ and <a href="#pi:projected-euler-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:projected-euler-equation">[pi:projected-euler-equation]</a> now bound the $L^2$ norm of $\ell\cdot U+k$ on this smaller ball by $C(\alpha+s)$. Affine nondegeneracy gives $$|\ell|+|k|\le C(\alpha+s).
 \label{pi:forcing-coefficient-bound}$$ Lemma <a href="#pi:radial-transport" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:radial-transport">22</a> consequently applies and yields $$\|w\|_{L^2(B_{2R}(z))}\le C(\alpha+s).
 \label{pi:projection-outer-bound}$$

We claim that $\alpha\le Cs$ with a uniform constant for all sufficiently late spaces. If no such constant and threshold existed, choose a sequence of increasingly late spaces and configurations with $\alpha/s\to\infty$. The positive floor in <a href="#pi:pinching-summability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:pinching-summability">[pi:pinching-summability]</a> makes $s>0$, so $\widehat w=w/\alpha$ is defined along this sequence. The norm on $B_R(z)$ is one, and <a href="#pi:projection-outer-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:projection-outer-bound">[pi:projection-outer-bound]</a> bounds its norm on $B_{2R}(z)$ uniformly. Interior harmonic compactness therefore gives local uniform and strong $W^{1,2}$ convergence on $B_{3R/2}(z)$ to a Euclidean harmonic function $w_\infty$; see (Ambrosio and Honda 2018) <a id="pm-cite-48"></a>[[AH18](#ref-AH18)]. Use a rotation so that the normalized columns $U$, centered at $z$, converge to the Euclidean coordinate column $X$.

<a id="pi:limiting-euler-equation"></a>
The coefficients $\ell/\alpha,k/\alpha$ are bounded by <a href="#pi:forcing-coefficient-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:forcing-coefficient-bound">[pi:forcing-coefficient-bound]</a>; pass to a convergent subsequence. The error $e_w/\alpha$ tends to zero in $L^2$. The gradients of $f^z$ converge strongly to the radial vector field $X$: the Poisson gradient error tends to zero, since $P_i(z)\le s^2\to0$, and the squared-distance gradient convergence was proved in Lemma <a href="#pi:radial-invariance" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:radial-invariance">20</a>. The normalized harmonic gradients have uniform interior bounds and converge strongly. Thus scalar products of these gradients pass to the limit in <a href="#pi:projected-euler-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:projected-euler-equation">[pi:projected-euler-equation]</a>, which becomes $$X\cdot\nabla w_\infty
 =w_\infty+\ell_\infty\cdot X+k_\infty
 \quad\hbox{on }B_{3R/2}(0).
 \label{pi:limiting-euler-equation}$$ One may first pass this identity against interior tests and then use the smoothness of the Euclidean harmonic limit.

Uniform convergence on a neighborhood of $\overline B_R(0)$ and convergence of the measures imply $$\|w_\infty\|_{L^2(B_R(0))}=1,\qquad
 \int_{B_R(0)}w_\infty\,\mathrm dX=0,\qquad
 \int_{B_R(0)}w_\infty X_j\,\mathrm dX=0\quad(1\le j\le n).$$ The boundary sphere has zero Euclidean measure, so restriction to the projection ball causes no loss in these limits. Expand $w_\infty$ in its harmonic Taylor series at zero. The operator $X\cdot\nabla-1$ multiplies the homogeneous term of degree $q$ by $q-1$. Equation <a href="#pi:limiting-euler-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:limiting-euler-equation">[pi:limiting-euler-equation]</a> therefore forces all terms of degree at least two to vanish. Analyticity makes $w_\infty$ affine on the ball. Its affine orthogonality would then give zero norm, a contradiction. This proves the claimed uniform bound $\alpha\le Cs$.

Finally Caccioppoli on the projection ball gives $\|\nabla w\|_{L^2(B_{R/2}(z))}\le Cs$. Since $B_{2L+4}(y)\subset B_{R/2}(z)$ and the constant $c_0$ does not affect gradients, this is <a href="#pi:sharp-projection-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:sharp-projection-equation">[pi:sharp-projection-equation]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-directional-pinching"></a>
<div id="pi:directional-pinching" class="proposition">

<a id="pi:directional-estimate"></a>
**Proposition 24** (Summable directional pinching). *For every fixed $L,C_{\mathrm{sep}}>1$, the functions $E_i$ may be chosen as in <a href="#pi:definition-E" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:definition-E">[pi:definition-E]</a>, with the uniform summability and positive floors in <a href="#pi:pinching-summability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:pinching-summability">[pi:pinching-summability]</a>, so that the following holds for all sufficiently late spaces. Define $$D_{i,y}=R_i(y)^{-1}
       \bigl[r_i^{2-n}M(u)(B_{Lr_i}(y))\bigr]R_i(y)^{-1}.$$ For every configuration <a href="#pi:center-configuration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:center-configuration">[pi:center-configuration]</a>, $$D_{i,y}[u(x)-u(z)]
 \le Cr_i^2\bigl(E_i(x)+E_i(z)\bigr).
 \label{pi:directional-estimate}$$ The same functions work simultaneously for all such centers and all dyadic scales. All enlargement constants are fixed before choosing the late-space threshold.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-pi-directional-pinching"><summary>Proof of stmt-pi-directional-pinching</summary>

<a id="pi:coefficient-recovery"></a>
*Proof.* Continue first in the rescaled units used in the preceding subsection, and choose $b$ from Lemma <a href="#pi:sharp-projection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:sharp-projection">23</a>. The normalization at $y$ is exactly $$\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_1(y)}
   (\langle\nabla U_j,\nabla U_k\rangle)_{j,k}\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m=\mathrm{Id}.$$ Consequently $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_1(y)}h\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 &=b+\mathop{\mathchoice
 {\displaystyle\int\mkern-18mu-}
 {\textstyle\int\mkern-15mu-}
 {\scriptstyle\int\mkern-12mu-}
 {\scriptscriptstyle\int\mkern-10mu-}}\nolimits_{B_1(y)}
       \langle\nabla U,\nabla(v-b\cdot U)\rangle\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
 =b+O(s).
\end{aligned}$$ Here the gradient bound for $U$ and <a href="#pi:sharp-projection-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:sharp-projection-equation">[pi:sharp-projection-equation]</a> control the last error. Equation <a href="#pi:constant-direction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:constant-direction">[pi:constant-direction]</a> then gives $$b=U(x)-U(z)+O(s)=\delta+O(s).
 \label{pi:coefficient-recovery}$$

<a id="pi:normalized-directional-mass"></a>
The harmonic function $w=v-c_0-b\cdot U$ has gradient energy at most $Cs^2$ on $B_{2L+4}(y)$. Choose a nonnegative test cutoff $\zeta$, equal to one on $B_L(y)$ and supported in $B_{2L+2}(y)$, with bounded Laplacian. Testing the definition of the nonnegative measure $M(w)$ gives <a id="pi:residual-mass"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 M(w)(B_L(y))
 &\le\frac12\int|\nabla w|^2\Delta\zeta\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m
    +\kappa r_i^2\int\zeta|\nabla w|^2\,\mathrm d\mathfrak m\notag\\
 &\le Cs^2.
 \label{pi:residual-mass}
\end{aligned}$$ Together with <a href="#pi:pair-poisson-bounds" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:pair-poisson-bounds">[pi:pair-poisson-bounds]</a>, positivity and polarization imply $M(b\cdot U)(B_L(y))\le Cs^2$. The normalized mass $M(U)(B_L(y))$ has bounded trace by Lemma <a href="#co:bochner-small" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:bochner-small">6</a>. Therefore <a href="#pi:coefficient-recovery" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:coefficient-recovery">[pi:coefficient-recovery]</a> implies $$M((\delta-b)\cdot U)(B_L(y))
 \le|\delta-b|^2\operatorname{tr}M(U)(B_L(y))\le Cs^2.$$ Another positive quadratic-form triangle inequality gives $$M(\delta\cdot U)(B_L(y))\le Cs^2.
 \label{pi:normalized-directional-mass}$$ In particular the $O(s)$ coefficient error has contributed $O(s^2)$ mass, including the possibly singular part of the Bochner measure.

We finish by recording the scale conversion explicitly. Return to the unrescaled units, set $T=R_i(y)^{-1/2}$ and $\delta u=u(x)-u(z)$. Under $d'=d/r_i$, $\mathfrak m'=r_i^{-n}\mathfrak m$ and $U=Tu/r_i$ up to an additive constant, the matrix-valued measure transforms as <a id="pi:matrix-measure-scaling"></a>$$M'(U)=r_i^{2-n}T M(u)T^T.
 \label{pi:matrix-measure-scaling}$$ Indeed the squared gradient matrix of $U$ in the primed metric is $TGT^T$, the Laplacian gains a factor $r_i^2$, and the lower curvature parameter becomes $\kappa r_i^2$. Meanwhile $\delta=U(x)-U(z)=T\delta u/r_i$. Hence, on the corresponding balls, $$\begin{aligned}
 M'(\delta\cdot U)(B_L'(y))
 &=r_i^{-n}\delta u^TR_i(y)^{-1}
       M(u)(B_{Lr_i}(y))R_i(y)^{-1}\delta u\\
 &=r_i^{-2}D_{i,y}[\delta u].
\end{aligned}$$ Applying <a href="#pi:normalized-directional-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:normalized-directional-mass">[pi:normalized-directional-mass]</a> and $s^2=E_i(x)+E_i(z)$ proves <a href="#pi:directional-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:directional-estimate">[pi:directional-estimate]</a>. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-pi-heat-interface"></a>
<div id="pi:heat-interface" class="remark">

*Remark 25* (Use with the heat-normalized matrices). The construction of $E_i$ and the radial argument used $R_i$ for the scalar coordinate norms and did not use the heat matrices. Lemma <a href="#co:heat-matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:heat-matrices">9</a>, specifically <a href="#co:QR-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:QR-comparison">[co:QR-comparison]</a>, identifies $Q_i$ and $R_i$ up to uniform relative $o(1)$. They may therefore be interchanged in bounded norm comparisons in the next section. This introduces only multiplicative constants; it adds no per-scale error term to the summable quantities in <a href="#pi:pinching-summability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:pinching-summability">[pi:pinching-summability]</a> or <a href="#pi:directional-estimate" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="pi:directional-estimate">[pi:directional-estimate]</a>. In particular the explicit floor $E_i(a)\ge C_E\eta2^{-\beta i}$ is available when choosing a single center that nearly minimizes $E_i$ on an active coordinate patch.

</div>

<a id="re:section"></a>
### Renormalization in the space of coordinate values

We now turn the harmonic coordinates into a bi-Lipschitz chart. The construction takes place in the ordinary Euclidean space of their values. In particular, the differentiations below do not involve a pointwise inverse of the differential of the harmonic map.

<a id="re:input-data"></a>
#### Notation and quantitative inputs

Write $r_i=2^{-i}$, $P_x=u(x)$, and $$\Omega=u(B_1(p)).$$ By Lemma <a href="#co:open-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:open-coordinates">7</a>, $\Omega$ is open and $u$ is a homeomorphism from $B_1(p)$ onto $\Omega$. We use the positive definite matrices $Q_i(x)$ and $H_i(x)=Q_i(x)^{-1}$ of Lemma <a href="#co:heat-matrices" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:heat-matrices">9</a>. For a positive definite matrix $H$, put $|\xi|_H=(\xi^T H\xi)^{1/2}$. Vectors in the source value space are measured with $H_i(x)$; covectors in that space are measured with $Q_i(x)$, its dual matrix. If $T$ is an $m$-linear map with covector values, our operator norm is $$\|T\|_{H\to Q}
 =\sup\bigl\{|T(\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_m)|_Q:
                 |\xi_a|_H\le1\ (1\le a\le m)\bigr\}.$$ The notation $\|T\|_{H\to\mathbb R^n}$ has the analogous meaning with the Euclidean target norm. For $m=0$ these conventions mean the target norm of the value. We denote ordinary Fréchet derivatives in value space by $\mathrm D^m$. Whenever a derivative norm at $P_x$ uses $H_i(x)$ or $Q_i(x)$, these matrices are frozen: they are not differentiated.

<a id="re:ellipsoid-input"></a>
The fixed constants from the preceding sections, including all required enlargements of balls, will be chosen before the final rescaling. We may then use a small parameter $\varepsilon>0$ tending to zero along that sequence of rescalings. Increasing this parameter by a fixed constant factor if necessary, the following bounds hold: <a id="re:monotone-metrics"></a>$$\label{re:monotone-metrics}
 H_i(x)\le H_{i+1}(x)\le(1+\varepsilon)H_i(x),
 \qquad Q_0(x)=\mathrm{Id}+o(1).$$ The matrices $R_i(y)^{-1}$ and $H_i(x)$ are uniformly comparable if $d(x,y)\le C r_i$, for each of the fixed constants $C$ used below. Moreover, normalized harmonic approximation gives the separation and central-ellipsoid conclusions of Lemmas <a href="#co:harmonic-normalization" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:harmonic-normalization">5</a> and <a href="#co:open-coordinates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:open-coordinates">7</a>. In particular, there are fixed $a>0$ and $C_{\mathrm{cmp}}\ge1$ such that, whenever the balls involved are interior, $$\label{re:ellipsoid-input}
 \{P:|P-P_x|_{H_i(x)}<a r_i\}
       \subset u(B_{r_i}(x)),
 \qquad H_i(y)\le C_{\mathrm{cmp}}H_i(x)
       \quad(d(x,y)\le r_i).$$ Here and below, the harmonic map is defined on the larger balls supplied by the preceding sections, even when an image in <a href="#re:ellipsoid-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:ellipsoid-input">[re:ellipsoid-input]</a> is not contained in $\Omega$.

<a id="re:patch-matrix"></a>
<a id="re:cumulative-input"></a>
For a fixed sufficiently large $L$, recall the positive semidefinite matrices $$\label{re:patch-matrix}
 D_{i,y}=R_i(y)^{-1}
       \bigl[r_i^{2-n}M(B_{Lr_i}(y))\bigr]R_i(y)^{-1}.$$ Lemma <a href="#co:cumulative-comparison" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="co:cumulative-comparison">14</a> applies to boundedly many centers at distance $O(r_i)$ from a testing point, with weights in $[0,1]$ and with at least one weight equal to one whose mass ball contains $B_{r_i}(x)$. With the notation $Z_i$ for the unweighted sum and $S_i$ for the weighted sum, it gives a fixed constant $C_*\ge1$ such that $$\label{re:cumulative-input}
 \begin{aligned}
 Z_i(x)&\le\varepsilon H_i(x),\\
 \mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i<k}Z_i(x)&\le C_*H_k(x),\\
 H_k(x)&\le C_*\left(\mathrm{Id}+\sum_{i<k}S_i(x)\right).
 \end{aligned}$$ Enlarging $C_*$ if needed, these statements include $k=0$.

<a id="re:pinching-input"></a>
<a id="re:directional-input"></a>
By Proposition <a href="#pi:directional-pinching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="pi:directional-pinching">24</a>, for these fixed choices of $L$ and center separations there are functions $E_i$ on a fixed domain containing $B_1(p)$ such that $$\label{re:pinching-input}
 \sup_{x\in B_1(p)}\sum_{i=0}^\infty E_i(x)\le\varepsilon,
 \qquad E_i(x)\ge e_i^*>0,$$ where $e_i^*$ is independent of $x$ at each fixed $i$, and $$\label{re:directional-input}
 D_{i,y}[P_x-P_z]
       \le C r_i^2\bigl(E_i(x)+E_i(z)\bigr)
 \quad\text{if }d(x,y),d(z,y)\le C_0r_i.$$ We use $D[\xi]=\xi^TD\xi$ for quadratic forms. The positive floor in <a href="#re:pinching-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:pinching-input">[re:pinching-input]</a> is supplied by the exponentially decreasing positive term in the construction of $E_i$. Such a term can always be added with arbitrarily small sum. It is used only to select fixed centers for the patches.

All later constants depend on dimension and the fixed preceding constants, but not on the scale, the testing point, or the number of scales. Constants denoted by $C_A$ may additionally depend on a bootstrap constant $A$ and on the jet constants fixed after $A$.

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Smooth fields with directional control

For each $i$ choose a maximal $r_i$-separated subset $\mathcal Y_i\subset B_1(p)$. Properness and local doubling imply that this family is finite; maximality implies that every point of $B_1(p)$ is at distance at most $r_i$ from some member.

<a id="re:bump-jets"></a>
Fix a smooth function $\psi:\mathbb R^n\to[0,1]$ equal to one on $\{|X|\le2\}$ and supported in $\{|X|<3\}$, with $$\label{re:bump-jets}
 \|\mathrm D^m\psi\|_{\infty}
       \le C_b^{m+1}(m!)^s\qquad(m\ge0)$$ for a fixed $s>1$. One may, for example, take $s=2$. To recall why such bounds are available, the function $e^{-1/t}$ for $t>0$, extended by zero for $t\le0$, has on each fixed bounded interval derivative bounds $C^{m+1}(m!)^2$. Cauchy’s estimate on a complex disk of radius a fixed small multiple of $t$ bounds its $m$th derivative by $m!(C/t)^m e^{-c/t}$, and optimization in $t$ gives this assertion. Products, integration, and composition with a fixed quadratic polynomial give a radial cutoff with the specified radii and the same factorial order, by the finite product and chain formulas.

Define $$\phi_{i,y}(P)=
 \psi\left(r_i^{-1}R_i(y)^{-1/2}(P-P_y)\right).$$ For $x\in B_1(p)$ let $$\mathcal A_i(x)=\{y\in\mathcal Y_i:P_x\in\operatorname{supp}\phi_{i,y}\}.$$ The harmonic separation estimates show that every member of $\mathcal A_i(x)$ satisfies $d(x,y)\le C_0r_i$ for a fixed $C_0$. Indeed, fix a growth exponent $\alpha=1/16$ in <a href="#co:slow-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-R">[co:slow-R]</a>. For $r_i\le t=d(x,y)\le1$, choose $j\le i$ with $t\le r_j<2t$. The separation estimate at scale $r_j$, followed by inverse matrix comparison in <a href="#co:slow-R" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="co:slow-R">[co:slow-R]</a>, gives $$|P_x-P_y|_{R_i(y)^{-1}}
 \ge c(r_i/r_j)^\alpha |P_x-P_y|_{R_j(y)^{-1}}
 \ge c r_i(t/r_i)^{1-\alpha}.$$ Membership in the cutoff support bounds the left side by $3r_i$, so $t/r_i$ is bounded. For $t>1$ in $B_1(p)$, the initial harmonic approximation at scale one gives the same conclusion by comparison with $R_0(y)$. Packing therefore gives $|\mathcal A_i(x)|\le N$ for a fixed $N$. For sufficiently small flatness, a net point with $d(x,y)\le r_i$ satisfies $|P_x-P_y|_{R_i(y)^{-1}}<2r_i$, so that $\phi_{i,y}(P_x)=1$. Choose $L>C_0+2$ in <a href="#re:patch-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:patch-matrix">[re:patch-matrix]</a>. In particular the mass ball of this weight-one center contains $B_{r_i}(x)$.

These facts justify the application of <a href="#re:cumulative-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:cumulative-input">[re:cumulative-input]</a> to <a id="re:active-sums"></a>$$\label{re:active-sums}
 Z_i(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal A_i(x)}D_{i,y},\qquad
 S_i(x)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal A_i(x)}
                     \phi_{i,y}(P_x)D_{i,y}.$$ We fix these sums from now on. They are used only pointwise as quadratic forms; no spatial differentiability of them is asserted.

<a id="re:fixed-center"></a>
For a patch let $$T_{i,y}=\{x\in B_1(p):P_x\in\operatorname{supp}\phi_{i,y}\}.$$ It is nonempty, since it contains $y$. All its points lie within $C_0r_i$ of $y$. By the positive floor in <a href="#re:pinching-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:pinching-input">[re:pinching-input]</a>, its infimum of $E_i$ is positive. Choose one point $z_{i,y}\in T_{i,y}$ satisfying $$\label{re:fixed-center}
 E_i(z_{i,y})<2\inf_{x\in T_{i,y}}E_i(x),
 \qquad c_{i,y}=P_{z_{i,y}}.$$ Thus a single fixed point satisfies $E_i(z_{i,y})\le2E_i(x)$ at every active testing point of the patch, including the support of every derivative of its cutoff. No continuity of $E_i$ or measurable selection is needed. Restricting the minimization to $T_{i,y}$ also keeps its center in the stated domain at the coarsest scales.

<a id="re:field-definition"></a>
The following is the covector field used in the iteration: $$\label{re:field-definition}
 v_i(P)=\sum_{y\in\mathcal Y_i}
           \phi_{i,y}(P)D_{i,y}(P-c_{i,y}).$$ It is a smooth field on all of value space, since the sum is finite.

<a id="stmt-re-field-estimates"></a>
<div id="re:field-estimates" class="lemma">

<a id="re:field-value"></a>
<a id="re:field-small-linear"></a>
<a id="re:field-diagonal"></a>
<a id="re:field-second"></a>
<a id="re:field-jets"></a>
**Lemma 26** (Field estimates). *At $P=P_x$, $x\in B_1(p)$, write $r=r_i$, $H=H_i(x)$, $Q=Q_i(x)$, $Z=Z_i(x)$, $S=S_i(x)$, and $E=E_i(x)$. There is a linear error $e(\xi)$ such that <a id="re:field-linear"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 r^{-1}|v_i|_Q&\le C\sqrt\varepsilon\sqrt E,
       \label{re:field-value}\\
 (\mathrm Dv_i)\xi&=S\xi+e(\xi),
       \label{re:field-linear}\\
 |S\xi|_Q&\le\sqrt\varepsilon\sqrt{Z[\xi]},
       &|e(\xi)|_Q&\le C\sqrt\varepsilon\sqrt E\,|\xi|_H,
       \label{re:field-small-linear}\\
 |\xi^Te(\xi)|&\le C\sqrt{Z[\xi]E}\,|\xi|_H,
       \label{re:field-diagonal}\\
 r|\mathrm D^2v_i(\xi,\xi)|_Q
       &\le C\left(\sqrt{Z[\xi]}\,|\xi|_H
                          +\sqrt E\,|\xi|_H^2\right).
       \label{re:field-second}
\end{aligned}$$ There is a fixed $C_v\ge1$ such that, in every order $m\ge0$, $$\label{re:field-jets}
 r_i^{m-1}\|\mathrm D^m v_i(P_x)\|_{H_i(x)\to Q_i(x)}
       \le\varepsilon C_v^{m+1}(m!)^s.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-field-estimates"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-field-estimates</summary>

<a id="re:center-energy"></a>
<a id="re:positive-matrix-bound"></a>
*Proof.* All sums in this proof are over $\mathcal A_i(x)$. Put $w_y=P_x-c_{i,y}$. Equation <a href="#re:directional-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:directional-input">[re:directional-input]</a> and the fixed choice <a href="#re:fixed-center" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:fixed-center">[re:fixed-center]</a> give $$\label{re:center-energy}
 D_{i,y}[w_y]\le C r^2E.$$ The same center-distance bounds and harmonic normalization give $|w_y|_H\le Cr$. Since $0\le D_{i,y}\le Z\le\varepsilon H$, conjugation by $H^{-1/2}$ gives $$\label{re:positive-matrix-bound}
 |D_{i,y}w|_Q^2\le\varepsilon D_{i,y}[w]
 \quad\text{and}\quad
 |\xi^TD_{i,y}w|^2\le D_{i,y}[\xi]D_{i,y}[w].$$ Here the second inequality is Cauchy–Schwarz for a positive semidefinite form and does not require its invertibility. Applying the first inequality to <a href="#re:center-energy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:center-energy">[re:center-energy]</a> and using the bounded number of summands proves <a href="#re:field-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-value">[re:field-value]</a>.

<a id="re:cutoff-derivative"></a>
Differentiation of <a href="#re:field-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-definition">[re:field-definition]</a> gives $$e(\xi)=\sum_y
          (\mathrm D\phi_{i,y})\xi\ D_{i,y}w_y.$$ By normalized cutoff bounds and matrix comparability, $$\label{re:cutoff-derivative}
 |(\mathrm D\phi_{i,y})\xi|\le Cr^{-1}|\xi|_H,
 \qquad
 |\mathrm D^2\phi_{i,y}(\xi,\xi)|\le Cr^{-2}|\xi|_H^2.$$ These inequalities and <a href="#re:positive-matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:positive-matrix-bound">[re:positive-matrix-bound]</a> prove the bound for $e$ in <a href="#re:field-small-linear" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-small-linear">[re:field-small-linear]</a>. Since $0\le S\le Z\le\varepsilon H$, the same conjugation gives $SQS\le\varepsilon S\le\varepsilon Z$, proving its bound for $S$. For the sharper diagonal bound, use the second inequality in <a href="#re:positive-matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:positive-matrix-bound">[re:positive-matrix-bound]</a> before summing: $$|\xi^Te(\xi)|
 \le C\sqrt E\,|\xi|_H\sum_y\sqrt{D_{i,y}[\xi]}
 \le C\sqrt{Z[\xi]E}\,|\xi|_H.$$ Finally, $$\mathrm D^2v_i(\xi,\xi)
 =\sum_y\left(
   \mathrm D^2\phi_{i,y}(\xi,\xi)D_{i,y}w_y
       +2(\mathrm D\phi_{i,y})\xi\ D_{i,y}\xi\right).$$ Equations <a href="#re:positive-matrix-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:positive-matrix-bound">[re:positive-matrix-bound]</a>–<a href="#re:cutoff-derivative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:cutoff-derivative">[re:cutoff-derivative]</a> bound the two sums by $Cr^{-1}\sqrt\varepsilon\sqrt E\,|\xi|_H^2$ and $Cr^{-1}\sqrt\varepsilon\sqrt{Z[\xi]}\,|\xi|_H$, respectively. Dropping $\sqrt\varepsilon\le1$ proves <a href="#re:field-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-second">[re:field-second]</a>.

For the isotropic estimates, $D_{i,y}\le\varepsilon H$ also gives $\|D_{i,y}\|_{H\to Q}\le\varepsilon$ and $|D_{i,y}w_y|_Q\le C\varepsilon r$. In order $m\ge1$, the product in <a href="#re:field-definition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-definition">[re:field-definition]</a> has only the term with $m$ derivatives on the cutoff and the $m$ terms with one derivative on the affine factor. By <a href="#re:bump-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:bump-jets">[re:bump-jets]</a>, the frozen source-norm comparison, and bounded overlap, their total norm is at most $$C\varepsilon r^{1-m}
 \left(C_1^{m+1}(m!)^s
                  +m C_1^m((m-1)!)^s\right).$$ Increase $C_v$ once to obtain <a href="#re:field-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-jets">[re:field-jets]</a>. The preceding value bound with $|w_y|_H\le Cr$ proves its $m=0$ case. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### The iteration and its derivative bounds

<a id="re:iteration"></a>
On the common open set $\Omega$ define, as long as the Jacobians are invertible there, $$\label{re:iteration}
 h_0(P)=P,
 \qquad J_i(P)=\mathrm Dh_i(P),
 \qquad h_{i+1}(P)=h_i(P)+J_i(P)^{-T}v_i(P).$$ The inverse transpose is chosen to turn the leading derivative of $v_i$ directly into a change of metric. To see both the gain and the error, put $W=J^{-T}v$ for a smooth map $h$ with $J=\mathrm Dh$. Differentiating $J^TJ^{-T}=\mathrm{Id}$ gives the exact identity <a id="re:metric-step"></a>$$\label{re:metric-step}
 \begin{aligned}
 |(J+\mathrm DW)\xi|^2-|J\xi|^2
   ={}&2\xi^T\mathrm Dv\,\xi
       -2\langle\mathrm D^2h(\xi,\xi),J^{-T}v\rangle\\
     &+|\mathrm DW\,\xi|^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ By the field estimates, $\mathrm Dv_i\,\xi=S_i\xi+e(\xi)$. The first term therefore supplies the desired positive increment $2S_i[\xi]$, up to the directional error. The middle term explains why we will estimate the diagonal derivative $\mathrm D^2h_i(\xi,\xi)$ by a scale recurrence. Bounds on all higher derivatives serve a different purpose: they keep the smooth update controlled despite its loss of one derivative.

<a id="re:metric-bootstrap"></a>
<a id="re:jet-bootstrap"></a>
We shall prove simultaneously a metric bound and all higher-derivative bounds. Choose $A\ge1$, subsequently fixed in terms of $C_*$, and fix $\sigma\ge\max\{1,s\}$. The desired bounds are $$\begin{aligned}
 A^{-1}H_i(x)&\le J_i(P_x)^TJ_i(P_x)\le A H_i(x),
      \label{re:metric-bootstrap}\\
 r_i^{m-1}\|\mathrm D^m h_i(P_x)\|_{H_i(x)\to\mathbb R^n}
       &\le B^{m-1}(m!)^{1+\sigma}\qquad(m\ge2),
      \label{re:jet-bootstrap}
\end{aligned}$$ where $B$ will depend on the fixed data and $A,\sigma$, but not on $\varepsilon,i,x,m$.

The next lemma shows that matrix inversion preserves the exponential base $B$ in these derivative bounds. A new factor growing exponentially with the order at each step would prevent the simultaneous induction.

<a id="stmt-re-inverse-majorant"></a>
<div id="re:inverse-majorant" class="lemma">

<a id="re:convolution-decay"></a>
<a id="re:inverse-jets"></a>
**Lemma 27** (Factorial convolution and inverse coefficients). *Fix $\sigma\ge1$ and put $L_0=1$ and $$L_m=(m+1)((m+1)!)^\sigma\qquad(m\ge1).$$ Then $$\label{re:convolution-decay}
 \delta_m:=\sum_{l=1}^{m-1}\frac{L_lL_{m-l}}{L_m}
       \longrightarrow0,
 \qquad
 \sum_{l=0}^m L_lL_{m-l}\le C_\sigma L_m.$$ Suppose a smooth matrix-valued function $J$ is invertible at a point and, in Euclidean operator norms at that point, $$\|J^{-1}\|\le\sqrt A,\qquad
 \frac{\|\mathrm D^mJ\|}{m!}\le B^mL_m\quad(m\ge1),
 \qquad A,B\ge1.$$ There is a constant $C_{A,\sigma}$, independent of $m$ and $B$, such that $$\label{re:inverse-jets}
 \frac{\|\mathrm D^m(J^{-1})\|}{m!}
       \le C_{A,\sigma}B^mL_m\qquad(m\ge0).$$ The assertion also holds for the inverse transpose.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-inverse-majorant"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-inverse-majorant</summary>

*Proof.* For $1\le l\le m-1$ the summand in the first expression of <a href="#re:convolution-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:convolution-decay">[re:convolution-decay]</a> is $$\frac{(l+1)(m-l+1)}{m+1}
 \left(\frac{(l+1)!(m-l+1)!}{(m+1)!}\right)^\sigma.$$ The two terms with $l=1,m-1$ are $O_\sigma(m^{-\sigma})$; the terms with $l=2,m-2$ are $O_\sigma(m^{-2\sigma})$. For $3\le l\le m-3$, the factorial ratio is at most $24/((m+1)m(m-1))$: its maximum in this interval occurs at an endpoint, as is seen by taking the ratio of consecutive terms. The first factor is at most $m+1$, and there are at most $m$ such terms. Their sum is therefore $O_\sigma(m^{2-3\sigma})$, which is $O_\sigma(m^{-\sigma})$ because $\sigma\ge1$. This proves $\delta_m\to0$; the finitely many small values of $m$ do not matter. Including the terms $l=0,m$ proves the second assertion of <a href="#re:convolution-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:convolution-decay">[re:convolution-decay]</a>.

<a id="re:inverse-recursion"></a>
Let $K=J^{-1}$. The multilinear product formula, followed by the operator norm inequality and division by $m!$, gives $$\frac{\|\mathrm D^m K\|}{m!}
 \le\sqrt A\sum_{l=1}^m
        \frac{\|\mathrm D^l J\|}{l!}
        \frac{\|\mathrm D^{m-l}K\|}{(m-l)!}.$$ In particular the binomial coefficients in the product formula have been removed exactly. After dividing out $B^m$, universal majorants $a_m$ for the positive-order coefficients satisfy $$\label{re:inverse-recursion}
 a_m\le A L_m+\sqrt A
                    \sum_{l=1}^{m-1}L_l a_{m-l}.$$ Choose $M=M(A,\sigma)$ so that $\sqrt A\,\delta_m\le1/2$ for $m\ge M$. The recursion for the finitely many orders $m<M$ gives finite universal bounds depending only on $A$ and $\sigma$. Choose $C_{A,\sigma}$ to dominate $\sqrt A$, $2A$, and all these bounds after division by $L_m$. If $a_q\le C_{A,\sigma}L_q$ for $q<m$, then <a href="#re:inverse-recursion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:inverse-recursion">[re:inverse-recursion]</a> gives, for $m\ge M$, $$\frac{a_m}{L_m}
       \le A+\sqrt A\,C_{A,\sigma}\delta_m
       \le A+\frac12 C_{A,\sigma}
       \le C_{A,\sigma}.$$ This proves <a href="#re:inverse-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:inverse-jets">[re:inverse-jets]</a> with a single constant for all orders; no factor $C_{A,\sigma}^m$ has been introduced. Transposition preserves Euclidean operator norms and the same derivative estimates. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-re-jet-step"></a>
<div id="re:jet-step" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (One step for all jets). *Choose $B\ge C_v$ sufficiently large and then $\varepsilon$ sufficiently small, depending on $A,\sigma,B$ and the fixed data. If <a href="#re:metric-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-bootstrap">[re:metric-bootstrap]</a> and <a href="#re:jet-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:jet-bootstrap">[re:jet-bootstrap]</a> hold at level $i$, the update <a href="#re:iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:iteration">[re:iteration]</a> satisfies <a href="#re:jet-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:jet-bootstrap">[re:jet-bootstrap]</a> at level $i+1$. This assertion does not require the new-level metric bound.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-jet-step"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-jet-step</summary>

*Proof.* Freeze $x$ and abbreviate $r=r_i$, $H=H_i(x)$, $Q=H^{-1}$. The linear source and target normalizations are $$\begin{aligned}
 P&=P_x+rH^{-1/2}X,\\
 \widetilde h(X)
   &=r^{-1}\bigl(h_i(P_x+rH^{-1/2}X)-h_i(P_x)\bigr),\\
 \widetilde v(X)&=r^{-1}H^{-1/2}v_i(P_x+rH^{-1/2}X).
 \end{aligned}$$ At $X=0$, $\widetilde J=\mathrm D\widetilde h=J_iH^{-1/2}$ has singular values between $A^{-1/2}$ and $A^{1/2}$, and $$\widetilde J^{-T}\widetilde v
           =r^{-1}J_i^{-T}v_i.$$ This identity checks the covector normalization in the update. All matrices in the change of variables are constant matrices.

<a id="re:increment-jets"></a>
For every $m\ge1$, <a href="#re:jet-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:jet-bootstrap">[re:jet-bootstrap]</a> gives $$\frac{\|\mathrm D^m\widetilde J(0)\|}{m!}
 \le\frac{B^m((m+1)!)^{1+\sigma}}{m!}
 =B^m L_m.$$ Lemma <a href="#re:inverse-majorant" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:inverse-majorant">27</a> therefore bounds the coefficients of $\widetilde J^{-T}$ by $C_{A,\sigma}B^mL_m$. On the other hand, <a href="#re:field-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-jets">[re:field-jets]</a> gives $$\frac{\|\mathrm D^q\widetilde v(0)\|}{q!}
       \le\varepsilon C_v^{q+1}(q!)^{s-1}
       \le\varepsilon C_v B^qL_q\qquad(q\ge0).$$ The product rule divided by $m!$ and <a href="#re:convolution-decay" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:convolution-decay">[re:convolution-decay]</a> now imply $$\label{re:increment-jets}
 \frac{\|\mathrm D^m(\widetilde J^{-T}\widetilde v)(0)\|}{m!}
       \le\varepsilon C_A B^m L_m.$$

To pass to next-scale jet units, note that $H_{i+1}(x)\ge H_i(x)$. Thus the norm of any multilinear map with source norm $H_{i+1}(x)$ is at most its norm with source norm $H_i(x)$. The change from $r_i$ to $r_{i+1}=r_i/2$ supplies the additional factor $2^{1-m}$. Relative to the allowance $B^{m-1}(m!)^{1+\sigma}$ at order $m\ge2$, the old contribution is at most $2^{1-m}\le1/2$, while <a href="#re:increment-jets" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:increment-jets">[re:increment-jets]</a> contributes at most $$\varepsilon C_A B\,2^{1-m}(m+1)^{1+\sigma}.$$ The supremum of $2^{1-m}(m+1)^{1+\sigma}$ over $m\ge2$ is finite. One sufficiently small choice of $\varepsilon$ therefore makes the new contribution at most one half in every order simultaneously. This proves the lemma. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
#### Diagonal second derivatives and the metric bootstrap

<a id="stmt-re-metric-iteration"></a>
<div id="re:metric-iteration" class="proposition">

**Proposition 29** (Uniform metric and jet bounds). *There are fixed $A,B$ and a sufficiently small flatness threshold such that the maps <a href="#re:iteration" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:iteration">[re:iteration]</a> are smooth on all of $\Omega$ for every finite $i$, their Jacobians are invertible there, and <a href="#re:metric-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-bootstrap">[re:metric-bootstrap]</a>–<a href="#re:jet-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:jet-bootstrap">[re:jet-bootstrap]</a> hold for all $x\in B_1(p)$ and every $i$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-metric-iteration"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-metric-iteration</summary>

<a id="re:first-inverse-cost"></a>
<a id="re:second-inverse-cost"></a>
*Proof.* We give the estimates under the induction hypothesis through level $k-1$ and then close that induction. Fix $x$ and a nonzero source vector $\xi$. Every expression in this proof is evaluated at $P_x$. Set $$q_i=|\xi|_{H_i(x)},\qquad
 b_i=\frac{r_i|\mathrm D^2h_i(\xi,\xi)|}{q_i},\qquad
 K_i=J_i^{-T},\qquad W_i=K_iv_i.$$ The metric bound gives $|K_iw|\le\sqrt A|w|_{Q_i(x)}$ for covectors $w$, since $(J_i^TJ_i)^{-1}\le A Q_i(x)$. The jet bounds through order three, the ordinary inverse derivative formula, and the duality of $H_i$ and $Q_i$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 |(\mathrm D_\xi K_i)w|
     &\le C_A\frac{q_i}{r_i}|w|_{Q_i(x)},
       \label{re:first-inverse-cost}\\
 |(\mathrm D_\xi^2 K_i)w|
     &\le C_A\frac{q_i^2}{r_i^2}|w|_{Q_i(x)}.
       \label{re:second-inverse-cost}
\end{aligned}$$ For completeness, after the frozen normalization in the preceding lemma, the first formula is $\mathrm D_\xi K_i=-K_i(\mathrm D_\xi J_i)^TK_i$. The second derivative is a sum of two products containing two first derivatives of $J_i$ and one product containing $\mathrm D_\xi^2J_i$. Bounds for $\mathrm D^2h_i$ and $\mathrm D^3h_i$, with the bounded normalized inverse, give exactly <a href="#re:first-inverse-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:first-inverse-cost">[re:first-inverse-cost]</a>–<a href="#re:second-inverse-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:second-inverse-cost">[re:second-inverse-cost]</a> after undoing the normalization. Thus these estimates are also valid for arbitrarily anisotropic $H_i$.

Twice differentiating $W_i$ gives all three types of terms: <a id="re:second-increment-terms"></a>$$\label{re:second-increment-terms}
 \mathrm D^2W_i(\xi,\xi)
   =K_i\mathrm D^2v_i(\xi,\xi)
     +2(\mathrm D_\xi K_i)(\mathrm Dv_i\xi)
     +(\mathrm D_\xi^2 K_i)v_i.$$ By Lemma <a href="#re:field-estimates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:field-estimates">26</a> and <a href="#re:first-inverse-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:first-inverse-cost">[re:first-inverse-cost]</a>–<a href="#re:second-inverse-cost" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:second-inverse-cost">[re:second-inverse-cost]</a>, multiplication by $r_i/q_i$ bounds each of these terms by a constant times $$f_i:=\sqrt{Z_i(x)[\xi]}+\sqrt{E_i(x)}\,q_i.$$ More explicitly, the first term uses <a href="#re:field-second" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-second">[re:field-second]</a>; the second uses $|\mathrm Dv_i\xi|_{Q_i}\le
C\sqrt\varepsilon f_i$; and the third uses $|v_i|_{Q_i}\le C\sqrt\varepsilon r_i\sqrt{E_i(x)}$. Because $q_{i+1}\ge q_i$ and $r_{i+1}=r_i/2$, the old second derivative contracts by at least a factor of one half. Hence <a id="re:diagonal-recurrence"></a>$$\label{re:diagonal-recurrence}
 b_{i+1}\le\tfrac12 b_i+C_A f_i,
 \qquad b_0=0.$$ Convolution with the sequence $1,1/2,1/4,\ldots$, whose sum is two, is bounded on $\ell^2$. Thus, for every finite $k$ covered by the induction hypothesis, <a id="re:diagonal-square-sum"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i<k}b_i^2
 &\le C_A\sum_{i<k}\left(Z_i(x)[\xi]+E_i(x)q_i^2\right)
       \notag\\
 &\le C_A H_k(x)[\xi].
       \label{re:diagonal-square-sum}
\end{aligned}$$ The last line uses <a href="#re:cumulative-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:cumulative-input">[re:cumulative-input]</a>, monotonicity $H_i\le H_k$, and <a href="#re:pinching-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:pinching-input">[re:pinching-input]</a>. In particular it does not require that the matrices $H_i$ remain bounded as $i\to\infty$.

<a id="re:squared-increment"></a>
The first derivative of the increment satisfies $$\Delta_iJ\,\xi:=(J_{i+1}-J_i)\xi
       =K_i(\mathrm Dv_i\xi)+(\mathrm D_\xi K_i)v_i.$$ The same estimates give the pointwise square bound $$\label{re:squared-increment}
 |\Delta_iJ\,\xi|^2
 \le C_A\varepsilon
           \left(Z_i(x)[\xi]+E_i(x)q_i^2\right).$$ Consequently their sum for $i<k$ is at most $C_A\varepsilon H_k(x)[\xi]$.

<a id="re:inverse-transpose-contraction"></a>
It remains to control the linear part of the increment of the squared length. The undifferentiated inverse gives $$\langle J_i\xi,K_i\mathrm Dv_i\xi\rangle
       =\xi^T\mathrm Dv_i\xi
       =S_i(x)[\xi]+\xi^Te_i(\xi).$$ For the differentiated inverse, direct matrix multiplication gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \langle J_i\xi,(\mathrm D_\xi K_i)v_i\rangle
 &=-\xi^T(\mathrm D_\xi J_i)^TJ_i^{-T}v_i
       \notag\\
 &=-\langle\mathrm D^2h_i(\xi,\xi),K_iv_i\rangle_{\mathbb R^n}.
       \label{re:inverse-transpose-contraction}
\end{aligned}$$ The inner product on the right is the ordinary Euclidean target inner product. Its absolute value is bounded by $C_A b_iq_i\sqrt{E_i(x)}$, using <a href="#re:field-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-value">[re:field-value]</a> and dropping the additional factor $\sqrt\varepsilon\le1$.

<a id="re:metric-error"></a>
