Now expand $|J_{i+1}\xi|^2-|J_i\xi|^2$, sum over $i<k$, and use <a href="#re:field-diagonal" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-diagonal">[re:field-diagonal]</a>, <a href="#re:squared-increment" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:squared-increment">[re:squared-increment]</a>, and <a href="#re:inverse-transpose-contraction" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:inverse-transpose-contraction">[re:inverse-transpose-contraction]</a>. The two linear error sums satisfy, respectively, $$\begin{aligned}
 \sum_{i<k}|\xi^Te_i(\xi)|
 &\le C\left(\sum_{i<k}Z_i(x)[\xi]\right)^{1/2}
          \left(\sum_{i<k}E_i(x)q_i^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le C\sqrt\varepsilon H_k(x)[\xi],\\
 \sum_{i<k}b_iq_i\sqrt{E_i(x)}
 &\le\left(\sum_{i<k}b_i^2\right)^{1/2}
          \left(\sum_{i<k}E_i(x)q_i^2\right)^{1/2}
 \le C_A\sqrt\varepsilon H_k(x)[\xi].
\end{aligned}$$ We have proved the quantitative cumulative error estimate $$\label{re:metric-error}
 \left|\,|J_k\xi|^2-|\xi|^2
                       -2\sum_{i<k}S_i(x)[\xi]\,\right|
       \le C_A(\sqrt\varepsilon+\varepsilon)H_k(x)[\xi].$$ Only the sum of $E_i$ has been used, never the sum of its square roots.

<a id="re:leading-metric-comparison"></a>
We now fix the constants and complete the simultaneous induction. By <a href="#re:cumulative-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:cumulative-input">[re:cumulative-input]</a> and $0\le S_i\le Z_i$, $$\label{re:leading-metric-comparison}
 C_*^{-1}H_k
       \le\mathrm{Id}+2\sum_{i<k}S_i
       \le2C_*H_k.$$ Choose $A\ge4C_*$, increasing it if necessary for the initial comparability of $H_0$ and $\mathrm{Id}$. Fix $\sigma,B$ as in Lemma <a href="#re:jet-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:jet-step">28</a>. Only now choose the data sufficiently flat that its smallness requirement holds and $$C_A(\sqrt\varepsilon+\varepsilon)<\frac1{2C_*}.$$ Then <a href="#re:metric-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-error">[re:metric-error]</a> and <a href="#re:leading-metric-comparison" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:leading-metric-comparison">[re:leading-metric-comparison]</a> imply <a href="#re:metric-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-bootstrap">[re:metric-bootstrap]</a> with slack at level $k$.

Initially $h_0$ is affine, so all its positive-order derivatives beyond the first vanish, and both bootstrap statements hold. Suppose they hold through level $i$. The pointwise inverse of $J_i$ exists everywhere on $\Omega$ and is smooth there; hence the update defines a smooth $h_{i+1}$ on this same open set. The proof of <a href="#re:metric-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-error">[re:metric-error]</a> at $k=i+1$ uses inverse and jet estimates only through level $i$: the error sum uses $b_j$ only for $j\le i$, whose recurrence involves still earlier increments. It therefore proves the metric bound and invertibility at level $i+1$ without assuming them. Lemma <a href="#re:jet-step" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:jet-step">28</a> proves all jet bounds at level $i+1$ using the old-level data. This is an induction on the finite scale index with an all-orders assertion at each step, and completes the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-auto-remark-1"></a>
<div class="remark">

*Remark 30*. The construction neither differentiates the fields $H_i$ nor assumes that they have a finite limit. It also does not require the maps $h_i$ themselves to be globally injective. Their ordinary Jacobians are invertible on the fixed open value-space domain, which is exactly what the update needs.

</div>

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Passage to an open bi-Lipschitz chart

<a id="stmt-re-limit-chart"></a>
<div id="re:limit-chart" class="proposition">

**Proposition 31** (The limiting chart). *For sufficiently flat data in the preceding construction, there is $\rho>0$ such that the uniform limit $$F(x)=\lim_{i\to\infty}h_i(u(x)),\qquad x\in B_1(p),$$ restricts to a bi-Lipschitz homeomorphism from $B_\rho(p)$ onto an open subset of $\mathbb R^n$. Its distance comparisons use the ambient distance $d$ restricted to $B_\rho(p)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-limit-chart"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-limit-chart</summary>

<a id="re:uniform-tail"></a>
*Proof.* From <a href="#re:field-value" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:field-value">[re:field-value]</a> and <a href="#re:metric-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:metric-bootstrap">[re:metric-bootstrap]</a>, $$|h_{i+1}(P_x)-h_i(P_x)|
       \le C_A\sqrt\varepsilon r_i\sqrt{E_i(x)}
       \le C_A\varepsilon r_i.$$ The geometric series gives uniform convergence on $B_1(p)$ and a tail estimate $$\label{re:uniform-tail}
 \sup_{x\in B_1(p)}|F(x)-h_i(P_x)|\le\tau r_i,
 \qquad \tau=C_A\varepsilon\longrightarrow0.$$ Each approximating map is continuous, so $F$ is continuous.

<a id="re:taylor-constant"></a>
<a id="re:choice-D"></a>
Let $K_2=B(2!)^{1+\sigma}$, the fixed second-derivative constant in <a href="#re:jet-bootstrap" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:jet-bootstrap">[re:jet-bootstrap]</a>. By <a href="#re:ellipsoid-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:ellipsoid-input">[re:ellipsoid-input]</a>, along any value-space segment contained in the central ellipsoid at $x$, with preimages in $B_{r_i}(x)\subset B_1(p)$, $$\label{re:taylor-constant}
 |\mathrm D^2h_i(P_y)(\zeta,\zeta)|
       \le\frac{K_2C_{\mathrm{cmp}}}{r_i}
                         |\zeta|_{H_i(x)}^2.$$ Put $K_T=K_2C_{\mathrm{cmp}}/2$. Choose a fixed $D\ge1$ so large that $$\label{re:choice-D}
 D>4/a,\qquad 4K_T/D\le1/(8\sqrt A).$$ These choices depend only on constants already fixed in the construction.

<a id="re:pair-normalization"></a>
<a id="re:choice-tail"></a>
Take a further sufficiently late rescaling, without changing those constants, so that the normalized harmonic approximation at the fixed scale ratio $D$ gives $$\label{re:pair-normalization}
 \tfrac12 d(x,z)\le|P_z-P_x|_{H_i(x)}\le2d(x,z)
 \quad\text{whenever }Dr_i^{-1}d(x,z)\in[1/2,1]$$ on the interior balls in use. To justify this precise consequence, normalized harmonic approximation controls distances in value space up to $o(1)r_i$ on the fixed ball; since the displayed distances are at least $r_i/(2D)$, its relative error tends to zero after $D$ has been fixed. The relative comparison of $Q_i$ with the normalization matrices preserves these bounds. Also require $$\label{re:choice-tail}
 4D\tau\le1/(8\sqrt A).$$

Set, for example, $\rho=(16D+4)^{-1}$. If $x,z\in B_\rho(p)$ are distinct and $t=d(x,z)$, then $t\le2\rho$. Choose a dyadic scale with $$Dt\le r_i<2Dt.$$ It has $r_i<4D\rho<1$, and $B_{r_i}(x)\subset B_1(p)$. Thus every pair in the final ball, not merely sufficiently generic pairs, is covered by an available scale. Write $\Delta=P_z-P_x$. Equation <a href="#re:pair-normalization" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:pair-normalization">[re:pair-normalization]</a> gives $t/2\le|\Delta|_{H_i(x)}\le2t$. By <a href="#re:choice-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:choice-D">[re:choice-D]</a>, $|\Delta|_{H_i(x)}<a r_i$. The entire straight segment $$P_x+s\Delta,\qquad 0\le s\le1,$$ therefore lies in the central ellipsoid in <a href="#re:ellipsoid-input" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:ellipsoid-input">[re:ellipsoid-input]</a>, and hence in $u(B_{r_i}(x))\subset\Omega$. In particular every point where a derivative is integrated has an interior preimage and obeys the matrix comparison used in <a href="#re:taylor-constant" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:taylor-constant">[re:taylor-constant]</a>.

<a id="re:final-bilipschitz"></a>
Taylor’s formula along this segment yields $$h_i(P_z)-h_i(P_x)=J_i(P_x)\Delta+\mathcal R,
 \qquad
 |\mathcal R|\le\frac{K_T}{r_i}|\Delta|_{H_i(x)}^2
       \le\frac{4K_T}{D}t\le\frac{t}{8\sqrt A}.$$ The metric bound gives $$\frac{t}{2\sqrt A}\le|J_i(P_x)\Delta|\le2\sqrt A\,t.$$ Finally the two tails in <a href="#re:uniform-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:uniform-tail">[re:uniform-tail]</a> have total size at most $2\tau r_i\le4D\tau t\le t/(8\sqrt A)$. Combining the last three inequalities proves, for every $x,z\in B_\rho(p)$, $$\label{re:final-bilipschitz}
 \frac{1}{4\sqrt A}d(x,z)
       \le|F(x)-F(z)|\le4\sqrt A\,d(x,z).$$ The case $x=z$ is immediate. In particular $F$ is injective there and has a continuous inverse onto its image.

The set $u(B_\rho(p))$ is open, by the previously established open coordinates. The continuous injective map $$F\circ u^{-1}:u(B_\rho(p))\longrightarrow\mathbb R^n$$ therefore has open image by invariance of domain (Hatcher 2002, Theorem 2B.3) <a id="pm-cite-49"></a>[[Hatcher02](#ref-Hatcher02)]. This proves the proposition. All scale choices used $t=d(x,z)$ in the ambient space; no intrinsic path distance on the chosen neighborhood was substituted. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-re-limit-chart-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-re-limit-chart</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Let $p$ be the prescribed $n$-regular point of the noncollapsed $\mathrm{RCD}(K,n)$ space. The preceding sections provide the geometric, harmonic, heat, and directional-pinching conclusions on sufficiently late rescalings at $p$. First fix the finitely many patch enlargement constants using only $n$. Choose the cutoff with $s=2$ and fix $\sigma=2$. The cumulative constant $C_*$, the bootstrap constants $A,B$, and then $D$ in <a href="#re:choice-D" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:choice-D">[re:choice-D]</a> consequently depend only on $n$. Choose a rescaling sufficiently late that all their flatness requirements, the simultaneous induction, and <a href="#re:choice-tail" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:choice-tail">[re:choice-tail]</a> hold. Proposition <a href="#re:limit-chart" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="re:limit-chart">31</a> gives an open ball about $p$ and a chart with the required two-sided distance bound on that rescaled space. If its metric is $d/r_*$, multiply the coordinate values of that chart by $r_*$ when returning to the original metric. Both sides of <a href="#re:final-bilipschitz" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="re:final-bilipschitz">[re:final-bilipschitz]</a> then scale by the same factor, while the domain remains an open neighborhood and the image remains an open subset of $\mathbb R^n$. Thus Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a> holds with $L_n=4\sqrt{A(n)}$. The required rescaling, and hence the radius of the neighborhood in the original metric, may depend on $X$ and $p$. ◻

</details>
